- a local behavior that requires a fact that the role can never know.

These failures should not be deferred to browser execution merely because a
runtime implementation could eventually notice them.

This does not mean Gesso should pretend to know dynamic domain facts in
advance.  A Request becoming claimed by another user after rendering remains a
runtime/domain event.  Static strictness applies only where the contradiction is
already present in the declared semantic structure.

*** Canonical authoring principle

Application authors should state semantic intent once whenever the system can
derive the rest.

A choreography should not require synchronized copies of the same semantic fact
in:

- operation metadata;
- view metadata;
- browser-plan names;
- transport declarations;
- route strings;
- server registries;
- bootstrap registries.

Where an independent physical choice genuinely exists, it may be declared
separately and checked for correspondence.  Otherwise the preferred direction
is:

#+begin_example
one semantic declaration
    |
    +--> derived local plan
    +--> derived binding metadata
    +--> derived/validated transport requirements
    +--> derived diagnostics
#+end_example

This principle does not collapse all layers into one macro.  It reduces
duplicated sources of semantic truth while preserving explicit architectural
boundaries.

#+LATEX: \newpage
** Choreographic operation types
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-choreographic-operation-contract
:END:

A choreographic operation type is richer than an ordinary function type.

An ordinary function type is roughly:

#+begin_example
A -> B
#+end_example

A choreographic operation type describes an entry into a distributed protocol
and the valid continuations from that entry.

It may include:

- ordinary value schemas;
- evolving values and their basis;
- roles participating in the operation;
- principal and authority requirements;
- facts known by each role on entry;
- provenance justifying those facts;
- facts on which roles agree;
- resources required by operations;
- facts and values produced;
- possible outcomes;
- provisional execution permissions;
- authority transitions;
- continuation requirements;
- compatibility/version requirements where a suspended projection may outlive a
  deployment.

The operation type should *not* duplicate the complete choreography by manually
listing every branch already represented by the choreography.

The choreography is the source of truth for control flow.

The type declaration supplies external semantic commitments that cannot or
should not be inferred, while analysis derives as much as possible from the
actual choreography.

For example, the declaration may state:

#+begin_src clojure
{::choreo/roles
 {:helper
  {:principal :user
   :actor :client}

  :request-authority
  {:authority :request}}

 ::choreo/inputs
 {:request-id
  {:schema :uuid
   :known-by #{:helper}
   :provenance :input}

  :observed-basis
  {:schema ObservedBasisClaim
   :known-by #{:helper}
   :provenance :input}}

 ::choreo/authority
 {:request-authority #{:request/claim}}

 ::choreo/optimism
 {:role :helper
  :projection :request/claim}}
#+end_src

This example declares role requirements, input knowledge, and the intended
optimistic realization. It does not grant claim authority to whoever sends a
matching role label. Trusted server admission must establish the concrete
principal, subject, and operation premises. The helper's input knowledge of an
=ObservedBasisClaim= is knowledge of the claimed token, not evidence that the
helper acquired the observation it names.

The names =ObservedBasisClaim= and =AuthoritativeBasis= are conceptual semantic
categories.  They do not require two unrelated physical token formats, nor do
they require a particular Clojure nominal-type implementation.  The browser may
carry the same underlying XTDB token bytes that trusted code later compares or
uses to construct a consistency requirement, while the semantic status of those
values remains different.

XTDB-specific representation belongs in the XTDB/Live consistency adapter rather
than being permanently baked into the generic Choreo type vocabulary.

The verifier derives knowledge changes, branch requirements, and continuation
structure from the choreography body.

The exact Clojure syntax remains tentative. These semantics are the intended
contract.

#+LATEX: \newpage
*** Operation identity is semantic, not transport identity

A choreographic operation has a stable semantic identity that is distinct from
the mechanism used to invoke it.

For example:

#+begin_example
:request/claim
#+end_example

is not synonymous with:

#+begin_example
POST /requests/123/claim
#+end_example

nor with:

#+begin_example
button id="claim-request"
#+end_example

nor with:

#+begin_example
browser plan index 17
#+end_example

The transport, view, and executable-plan coordinates are realizations of the
semantic operation.  They may change across refactors or deployments without
changing the operation's meaning, provided compatibility rules permit the
change.

This distinction is required so that application closure can later verify
correspondence instead of mistaking physical equality for semantic identity.

A stable operation name is also distinct from the normalized semantic identity
of its contract. Keeping =:request/claim= unchanged while changing a relied-upon
authority rule, input requirement, outcome, or postcondition does not preserve
dependent validity merely because the name stayed the same. Conversely, a
transport refactor need not change the semantic contract if its checked
realization and compatibility obligations remain satisfied.

*** Semantic categories at operation boundaries

Operation APIs must preserve distinctions whose accidental substitution would
violate a semantic invariant even when the underlying Clojure representations
are identical or structurally compatible.

Representative distinctions include:

- =ObservedBasisClaim= versus trusted =AuthoritativeBasis=;
- semantic =CommandId= versus runtime =ExecutionId=;
- provisional versus authoritative observations;
- raw declarations versus checker-admitted declarations;
- raw certificate data versus checker-accepted certificate evidence.

The requirement is semantic non-interchangeability through the supported API.
The design does not mandate that every such category be implemented as a
=defrecord=, Java class, wrapper object, or other nominal runtime type.  Permitted
realizations include:

- nominal or opaque host-language values;
- smart constructors with inaccessible or conventionally private construction;
- checker-produced admitted values;
- explicit tagged data whose use is guarded by the appropriate admission path;
- an existing Gesso representation that already prevents the accidental
  substitution.

The choice should be the smallest mechanism that makes the wrong ordinary call
difficult and the resulting diagnostic precise.  A representation technique is
not valuable merely because it looks more typed.

Ordinary downstream application code should normally author domain values and
semantic declarations, not manually wrap every UUID, basis token, command, or
projection in framework ceremony.  Gesso should perform category establishment
at elaboration, checking, authoritative acquisition, model, or runtime
boundaries wherever it can derive the distinction itself.

For example, a useful public failure is conceptually:

#+begin_example
boundary:                trusted claim preparation requiring an acquired basis
requires:                AuthoritativeBasis for the declared scope
received:                unadmitted ObservedBasisClaim
missing step:            trusted authoritative acquisition / admission
#+end_example

rather than accepting the same-shaped value and relying on a comment to explain
why the caller should not have done so. The diagnostic applies at the boundary
that requires the acquired basis. A command intake may legitimately accept an
=ObservedBasisClaim= as weaker context; it must not demand that the browser
manufacture server-trusted authority before submitting a command.

The category and admission rules are defined in
[[#req-semantic-admission][Part II]]. When the supplied category cannot be
established, diagnostics report an unknown or unadmitted value rather than
trusting a client-supplied category label.

A nominal host-language value also does not retain trusted status merely because
it was serialized and reconstructed.  Crossing an adversarial wire boundary
returns the value to an untrusted representation until the relevant verifier,
authenticator, decoder, or trusted acquisition path re-establishes the stronger
semantic category.

This is primarily an accidental-misuse boundary inside cooperating application
code.  It does not claim that deliberately hostile trusted host code is unable
to bypass Gesso, import lower-level implementation namespaces, or directly use a
database capability it already possesses.

*** Operation contracts export a semantic interface

An operation type should expose a normalized semantic interface containing only
facts that downstream reasoning is allowed to rely on.

Depending on the operation, that interface may include:

- input value types;
- required entry knowledge;
- required provenance;
- role participation;
- principal/authority constraints;
- model operation identity;
- possible semantic outcomes;
- values/facts produced;
- authority changes;
- resource requirements;
- provisional permissions;
- settlement requirements;
- continuation compatibility;
- named assumptions and guarantees.

It should not include arbitrary implementation details merely because those
details are easy to serialize.

For example, the operation contract may establish:

#+begin_example
operation:
    :request/claim

requires:
    helper knows Request identity
    for the declared optimistic realization:
        helper has acquired the required scoped authoritative Request projection
    trusted server binds authenticated principal
    request authority may invoke public request.core/claim

outcomes:
    :confirmed
    :reconciled
    :rejected
    :already-incorporated
#+end_example

The outcome names above describe authoritative/model semantic dispositions.
They do not imply that every participant can immediately establish which
disposition occurred.  Participant-relative uncertainty and command-specific
reconciliation are specified in the command/failure layer that follows this
section. The supported operation must define which dispositions actually exist,
their payloads, and the predicates or model outcomes that justify them. This
illustrative list does not require every model to implement all four outcomes.
An unknown participant-relative outcome is not silently converted into any of
these authoritative dispositions.

The semantic interface does not encode incidental realization details such as:

#+begin_example
specific Ring handler var
specific Reitit route index
specific DOM node id
specific XTDB Java class
specific browser bundle offset
#+end_example

Those belong to later realization layers unless independently required by
runtime semantics.

*** External commitments versus derived structure

Operation declarations should state only semantic commitments that cannot or
should not be safely inferred.

The verifier/projector should derive:

- reachable continuations;
- behavior-sensitive branch obligations;
- knowledge propagation caused by declared communication;
- terminal paths;
- local plan structure;
- resource obligations that follow from the body.

It should not require the author to restate those facts manually in metadata.

Redundant declarations are dangerous because each copy may remain individually
plausible while disagreeing with the others.

The preferred rule is:

#+begin_quote
Declare external semantic commitments; derive internal control-flow facts from
the choreography body.
#+end_quote

*** Availability is not authorization

An operation may be present in a model or choreography registry without being
currently executable by a particular principal.

Likewise, a prepared or bound operation may be rendered as an affordance without
becoming durable authorization.

These distinctions must remain explicit:

#+begin_example
operation exists
    !=
operation is valid in this choreography state

operation is valid in this choreography state
    !=
current authenticated principal is authorized now

view renders operation capability
    !=
browser possesses authority
#+end_example

The trusted side re-establishes dynamic authorization at execution and protects
the relevant model premises at the authoritative transition boundary where
intervening changes matter. A rendered affordance, a successful earlier check,
and a valid operation contract do not reserve future eligibility.

This separation lets Gesso reason strongly about operation identity and
distributed protocol structure without pretending to statically prove current
business facts that can change between render and commit.

*** Malformed operation type versus wrong business policy

The unified design distinguishes structural/type malformedness from product
correctness.

If a declaration says that =:request/status= is an enum and a rule attempts:

#+begin_src clojure
(< request.status 10)
#+end_src

the expression is malformed relative to its semantic types and should be
rejected.

If the application states:

#+begin_src clojure
(= request.status :open)
#+end_src

when product policy actually intended another valid status test, the expression
may be perfectly well formed while encoding the wrong policy.

Gesso should make the former difficult or impossible to certify.  It cannot
infer the latter merely from syntax.

This distinction becomes more explicit in the later compositional-proof part,
where typed business-rule expressions and certified semantic interfaces are
defined in detail.  It is already a language-design constraint here because
Choreo must not confuse "type checks" with "is the policy humans intended."

*** A valid rule may still be inadmissible at a distributed program point

Intrinsic rule validity is not enough.

For example:

#+begin_example
rule:
    (= request.private-review :approved)

intrinsic type:
    Boolean
    VALID

browser use before browser knows request.private-review:
    INVALID AT THIS PROGRAM POINT
#+end_example

A distributed use of a rule therefore creates obligations beyond ordinary type
checking, including:

- the role knows every required fact;
- required provenance is available;
- trust or authority requirements are satisfied;
- optional values have been refined before use;
- authoritative-basis requirements are met where the rule depends on
  authoritative observation.

This is one of the central reasons Choreo needs a semantic type/knowledge layer
richer than ordinary function typing.

*** Operation declarations should remain inspectable data

The ordinary authoring surface may eventually include macros or helpers, but the
semantic representation after elaboration should remain explicit enough to:

- inspect;
- normalize;
- verify;
- project;
- fingerprint semantically;
- explain on failure;
- compare against dependent declarations.

Convenient syntax must not create an opaque second semantics.

Normalization must preserve the information needed to identify the owning
declaration and explain failed obligations without making source coordinates
part of semantic identity when they are diagnostic-only. The compiler or
elaborator must not synthesize an undeclared trusted assertion simply to make a
knowledge, authority, or continuation obligation pass.

The desired direction is:

#+begin_example
pleasant author syntax
        |
        v
explicit normalized semantic data
        |
        +--> verification
        +--> projection
        +--> diagnostics
        +--> later certification
#+end_example

** Malli as the value-schema substrate
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-value-schema-boundaries
:END:

Malli should provide the ordinary value-shape layer underneath choreographic
types.

A choreographic value may conceptually carry:

#+begin_src clojure
{:schema ClaimCommand
 :known-by #{:helper :request-authority}
 :agreement #{#{:helper :request-authority}}
 :provenance :communicated}
#+end_src

or:

#+begin_src clojure
{:schema RequestProjection
 :known-by #{:helper}
 :provenance :authoritative
 :basis AuthoritativeBasis}
#+end_src

Malli is responsible for questions such as:

- is this a UUID?
- does this map contain the required keys?
- is this settlement one of the allowed values?
- does this intermediate machine context have the promised shape?

Malli is not responsible for questions such as:

- did this value come from a trusted actor rather than merely having a valid
  shape?
- may this helper claim this Request?
- is this Organization currently open?
- does this principal have authority?
- is this stale basis still semantically compatible with the operation?
- did communication actually establish knowledge?
- did an authoritative observation justify the actor's knowledge?
- does this branch satisfy the global choreography?

Those remain domain, FX, Choreo, or authority/progression concerns.

Runtime validators should be compiled and reused for the admitted schema and
registry definitions whose identities they reference. If those bindings belong
to an admitted assembly generation, validator use must remain coherent with
that assembly generation. A relevant schema or registry change requires an
appropriately recompiled or independently validated compatible validator; an
old compiled validator must not silently enforce an obsolete contract.
Validator correspondence does not depend on a browser execution identity or
resource-instance counter.

The default design should validate important runtime boundaries in production,
including:

- externally supplied choreography inputs;
- message payloads;
- authoritative observation/projection envelopes where they cross a runtime
  boundary;
- FX input/output boundaries;
- projected-machine state transitions;
- settlements;
- provisional/canonical protocol payloads.

The successful path should use compiled boolean validators. Rich Malli
explanations should be constructed only on failure.

The default should be to keep these validations enabled in production and
optimize only when measurement shows a real hot path. Measurement alone does
not justify removing enforcement of a required premise. An optimization must
preserve the contract, for example by avoiding redundant checks after a trusted
admission boundary while retaining checks where untrusted or dynamic values
can enter.

*** Shape validation is necessary but not semantic certification

Successful runtime validation establishes conformance to the particular schema
under the validator and predicate contracts being relied upon. It is classified
as runtime enforcement, not automatically as a formal theorem. A custom
predicate's name does not establish an authority or provenance claim that its
implementation never checks.

It does not prove:

- that a browser supplied the value honestly;
- that the value has authoritative provenance;
- that the current principal may use it;
- that another actor knows the same value;
- that a model operation may be invoked;
- that a choreography branch is globally legal.

This is not a limitation to work around.  It is a deliberate separation of
correctness scopes.

The unified design therefore treats ordinary value schemas as one layer in a
larger semantic interface:

#+begin_example
value shape
    |
    v
semantic type / refinement
    |
    v
knowledge + provenance
    |
    v
authority + operation contract
    |
    v
choreographic admissibility
#+end_example

A success at one layer cannot be silently promoted into a stronger proposition
owned by a later layer.

The same rule applies when a stronger semantic category happens to use a nominal
host-language representation.  Malli may validate the fields inside an
=AuthoritativeBasis= representation, for example, but shape validation does not
establish that the value came through the trusted authoritative acquisition
path.  Conversely, an admitted value may still use ordinary immutable Clojure
data internally.  What matters is the checked construction/admission relation,
not whether the physical carrier is a record or a map.

A discriminator such as =:type :verified= is therefore useful only as data unless
the supported API also establishes why callers are entitled to rely on that
category.

*** Closed value boundaries are the default

Where data crosses a semantic boundary, the declared schema should normally
describe a closed payload.

Closedness is especially important for:

- client commands;
- choreography messages;
- provisional-state envelopes;
- settlement envelopes;
- authoritative projection metadata;
- FX inputs and outputs.

An open map is not harmless merely because consumers ignore unknown keys.
Unexpected fields can become an accidental covert semantic channel when later
code begins to read them.

When an application genuinely needs an open payload, that must be explicit and
its semantic treatment visible to verification and diagnostics. Transporting
opaque extension data need not grant it first-class knowledge status. If a
program wishes to branch on an extension field or consume it as authority,
correlation, or an operation input, that use requires a declared contract and
the appropriate admission.

Semantic payload closure is distinct from rejecting every byte of physical
transport metadata. The decoder may separate declared payload from transport
metadata through an explicit contract. It must reject undeclared semantic
fields at a closed boundary or keep explicitly permitted nonsemantic data
outside the semantic context; it must not silently merge it into that context.

*** Runtime validation preserves static premises

Static reasoning depends on runtime values continuing to satisfy the contracts
under which the reasoning was performed.

Consequently, runtime checks are not "extra tests" after proof.  They are
enforcement of proof premises where untrusted or dynamic values enter.

Representative boundaries include:

#+begin_example
browser bytes
    -> command schema validation
    -> trusted operation lookup

external read result
    -> projection-envelope validation
    -> trusted acquisition / scope / provenance checks
    -> justified knowledge establishment

FX implementation result
    -> declared output validation
    -> next semantic step

settlement payload
    -> settlement schema validation
    -> declared source / command / owner correlation checks
    -> admitted browser transition
#+end_example

If a runtime premise fails, execution must reject or recover according to the
declared protocol.  It must not continue on the theory that static verification
made runtime validation unnecessary.

*** Validators should be reusable and diagnostic failure should be rich

The successful path should use precompiled validators where possible.

The failure path should preserve enough information to explain:

- which semantic value failed;
- which schema or refinement was expected;
- where the value entered;
- which operation or transition depended on it;
- whether failure concerns shape, provenance, authority, or another layer.

A raw Malli explanation may be part of this evidence, but downstream error
reporting should not force users to reconstruct the semantic context from a
low-level schema mismatch alone.

*** Schema changes participate in semantic compatibility

A schema is not merely runtime validation configuration when another semantic
artifact relies upon its meaning.

Changes such as:

- removing an enum member;
- changing required versus optional;
- changing cardinality;
- changing a field's nominal semantic type;
- changing a closed variant set;

may invalidate dependent operation, rule, or choreography artifacts.

Later proof/certificate work therefore assigns semantic identities to normalized
interfaces rather than source files. These operational schema requirements do
not depend on completion of the later certificate machinery. The language/model
boundary must nevertheless expose those semantic dependencies instead of hiding
them inside arbitrary host-language code.

** Projection and branch knowledge
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-projection-branch-knowledge
:END:

Projection creates a local machine for each role.

Projection must preserve whatever mechanism is required to justify local
behavior.

Sometimes that mechanism is communication.

Sometimes the actor already has justified entry knowledge.

Sometimes the relevant fact is learned from authority outside the current
execution and enters a later choreography as authoritative input knowledge.

The projector/verifier must therefore reason in terms of knowledge requirements,
not merely message presence.

A role may branch only when it knows enough information to choose the branch.

The verifier should use behavior-sensitive choice analysis:

- if a role behaves differently between two branches, it must know the
  discriminator or an equivalent fact before its behavior diverges;
- the required knowledge may be justified through communication, authoritative
  observation, input knowledge, declared derivation, or explicit assertion;
- if its behavior is equivalent across the branches, it does not need to learn
  the choice merely because it is syntactically mentioned.

This is intentionally more precise than a simple "every role mentioned in either
branch must know the condition" rule.

For example:

#+begin_example
authority chooses approved/rejected

helper behavior differs afterward
        |
helper must know enough to distinguish the continuations
#+end_example

That requirement does not imply a dedicated authority-to-helper choreography
message if the helper can instead obtain the required approval fact through a
declared, authorized authoritative observation that satisfies the same branch
obligation.

The substitution must preserve subject, scope, provenance, and the relationship
to the decision being continued. Reading a currently approved Request does not
necessarily establish which earlier command was approved or which historical
branch was selected. If later model changes can erase or supersede the needed
fact, the protocol needs a suitable retained outcome, receipt, communication,
or different continuation contract. The projector must not invent that history
or treat an arbitrary later read as equivalent evidence.

Messages should be closed by default.

A message contract declares the fields that may cross the boundary. Arbitrary
extra fields should not be merged into choreography context.

An explicit =:open-payload? true= may exist for cases that genuinely require an
open payload, but closed contracts are the default.

Within the admitted choreography semantics, a sender must have justified
knowledge of every semantic fact it transmits. Receipt from an adversarial
participant still requires the trusted-boundary treatment defined in
[[#req-knowledge-provenance][the knowledge model]].

Receiving a message should only introduce the declared communicated values into
the receiving context.

Projection must not erase communication that is genuinely required to justify
knowledge.

Conversely, projection should not manufacture communication merely because the
global choreography contains a choice when the target role's behavior does not
depend on it or its knowledge is established through another valid path.

*** Projection is a semantic compilation boundary

Projection is not a convenience transformation that happens to produce smaller
maps.

It is the boundary at which the global specification is turned into the local
information and actions available to a role.

A correct projector must therefore preserve every fact required to justify
local behavior, including when relevant:

- message schemas;
- correlation identity;
- entry knowledge;
- knowledge-producing communication;
- authoritative-observation requirements;
- branch discriminators;
- provenance requirements;
- resource ownership;
- failure/terminal structure;
- executable guards required by local semantics.

At the same time, it should omit global facts that the role neither knows nor
needs.

The target is neither "copy the whole global machine to everyone" nor "erase
everything inconvenient."  It is a justified local program.

*** Projection cannot create knowledge

A projector may rearrange or encode information justified by the global
choreography.  It may not manufacture a fact merely because the local machine
would be easier to implement if the fact were present.

Bad:

#+begin_example
global:
    only authority knows :approved?

project browser:
    include :approved? in browser context anyway
#+end_example

Good:

#+begin_example
global:
    authority communicates approved outcome
or
    browser performs authorized authoritative observation

project browser:
    local branch becomes possible after that knowledge path
#+end_example

The same principle applies to provenance.  Projected data cannot silently
upgrade:

#+begin_example
asserted -> derived
communicated -> authoritative
client supplied -> trusted principal identity
#+end_example

Projection preserves or weakens claims according to explicit semantics; it does
not strengthen them by serialization.

*** Behavior-sensitive choice is the central branch rule

A role needs to know a choice only when its observable local behavior depends on
that choice. The required knowledge must exist before the first differing
behavior, not merely become available eventually.

This avoids both extremes:

- projecting too little and leaving a role unable to decide its next behavior;
- overcommunicating every global discriminator to every mentioned role.

The analysis should compare continuations semantically rather than merely by
source syntax.

If two global branches project to equivalent local behavior for role R, R need
not learn the branch solely because R appears somewhere inside both branches.

If they diverge, the projector/verifier must establish a valid knowledge path
before the divergence.

**** Supported equivalence and conservative rejection

Behavior-sensitive checking must define the local equivalence relation used by
its supported language subset. The observables include whichever messages,
effects, authoritative invocations, resource actions, and terminal or failure
outcomes the semantic contract distinguishes. Relevant ordering and timing
constraints must not disappear merely because the final values happen to match.

Permitted internal steps may be abstracted only under an explicit semantic
relation. The checker is not required to decide equivalence of arbitrary
Clojure functions or unrestricted programs.

When the supported analysis cannot establish equivalence or a sufficient
knowledge path, it must reject or report an unresolved obligation at the
appropriate admission boundary. It must not silently assume equivalence,
disclose a private discriminator, or introduce an undeclared message to make
projection succeed. An explicit supported communication path or visible trusted
assumption may be authored where permitted by the relevant contract.

**** Trace preservation and progress are separate obligations

Excluding forbidden local traces is a safety claim. It does not by itself prove
that a projected program can perform every required interaction, avoid deadlock,
or eventually complete. A projection that admits no useful behavior must not
satisfy the required operation merely because its empty behavior is safe.

Each claimed projection result must state the global/local behavior relation
and the entry and environmental premises under which it applies. Required
realizability, terminal behavior, and progress obligations must be discharged
with the appropriate evidence. Liveness remains conditional on its named
delivery, availability, fairness, or application-action assumptions.

These obligations supplement, rather than replace, the independent application
capability acceptance defined in Part I.

*** Message payload closure protects knowledge boundaries

Closed messages are not only a schema preference.  They are a knowledge-flow
boundary.

If a message declares:

#+begin_src clojure
{:request-id UUID
 :outcome ClaimOutcome}
#+end_src

Here =:request-id= identifies the domain Request involved in the Claim outcome;
it is not =:transport-request-id=, which identifies a transport attempt.
Receiving this message may establish only the declared communicated facts.

The receiver does not acquire arbitrary sender context, and the implementation
must not merge unknown payload keys into machine context by convenience.

An explicitly open payload must follow the extension-data contract from the
[[#req-value-schema-boundaries][value-schema boundary]]. Openness alone does not
grant undeclared fields semantic knowledge status or permission to influence a
branch. Any weaker guarantee actually selected remains visible in the result.

*** Projection preserves necessary communication but does not worship messages

Communication is one way to establish knowledge, not the definition of
knowledge.

A projector must preserve a message when that message is what justifies another
role's behavior.

It must not insert a redundant direct message merely because one actor's choice
eventually affects another actor if the other actor can instead obtain the
necessary fact by:

- justified entry knowledge;
- authoritative observation;
- deterministic derivation;
- another declared knowledge path.

This matters deeply for Gesso's web architecture because durable business facts
often rendezvous naturally through model authority and reread rather than
long-lived direct participant messaging.

*** Local executable guards belong in the executable projection

Proof derivations and global proof trees do not belong in the production
browser.

However, a local predicate or guard whose result is required to execute the
projected semantics is runtime-semantic data and must remain in the executable
projection.

The distinction is:

#+begin_example
"this callback may resume execution E only while its declared resource
 instance I retains the required ownership for E"
    -> executable admission guard if needed at runtime

"here is the proof tree explaining why that guard is sufficient"
    -> diagnostic/proof artifact
#+end_example

The guard identifies the execution and resource instance required by this
callback's admission contract; it is not a universal generation comparison.
Shared fragment or subscription events use their own declared ownership checks.
Passing an ownership guard does not by itself establish the knowledge,
provenance, or authority required for the resumed branch.

The executable semantic core evaluates runtime guards as part of the admitted
transition semantics. A proof kernel or dedicated semantic checker establishes
its stated judgment about those semantics; it does not replace the runtime
admission check or supply missing runtime facts.

The production machine must never depend on an optional proof sidecar to decide
a transition.

*** Projection failure should identify the missing justification

When projection cannot produce a legal local machine, diagnostics should explain
the actual semantic deficit.

Useful failure information includes:

- role;
- global state/transition;
- local behaviors that diverge;
- required discriminator;
- facts currently known;
- available provenance;
- missing knowledge path;
- candidate communication or authoritative-observation paths when derivable.

A generic "projection failed" error throws away precisely the information that
the verifier already computed.

*** Host-language callbacks may not bypass projected semantics

A local runtime may invoke host-language functions to realize declared local
effects, but arbitrary callbacks must not become an alternate transition path.

In particular:

#+begin_example
projected machine
    -> declared local effect
    -> host implementation
    -> normalized completion/failure
    -> projected machine
#+end_example

is compatible with the design.

But:

#+begin_example
host callback
    -> mutate choreography state directly
#+end_example

reopens the proof and knowledge boundary.

The common boundary is
[[#req-owner-relative-event-admission][owner-relative event admission]] in Part II.
Part VI develops its browser implementation. An execution-private effect
completion must not bypass that boundary, and a declared shared event must use
its actual fragment or subscription owner rather than an invented execution.

** Long logical workflows and short runtime executions
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-durable-workflow-boundary
:END:

A choreography may describe a logical workflow that spans human-scale time.

That does not imply that one Aleph node should retain a suspended server-side
machine in process memory for hours or days.

For example, a business flow may conceptually involve:

#+begin_example
request approval
        |
wait
        |
approval granted
        |
continue
#+end_example

A robust HumanHelp realization may instead be:

#+begin_example
short choreography E1
    |
commit authoritative :approval/pending
    |
E1 terminates

...minutes or hours...

approver observes pending authority
    |
short choreography E2
    |
commit authoritative :approval/approved
    |
E2 terminates

requestor later observes approved authority
    |
short choreography E3 if further action is needed
#+end_example

The durable wait lives in model state.

Subject to the authoritative store's declared durability and recovery
assumptions, the durable workflow fact can survive:

- web-node failure;
- deployment;
- load-balancer rerouting;
- client disconnect;
- browser suspension;
- long offline periods.

The initial Gesso choreography runtime therefore does not promise durable
persistence of arbitrary suspended server-local executions.

If a future application genuinely needs durable workflow-machine continuations,
that should be an explicit opt-in capability with its own persistence, ownership,
versioning, and recovery semantics.

It must not silently become a requirement of ordinary choreography.

*** Durable waiting belongs in authoritative state by default

Human-scale waiting is normally represented as durable domain state rather than
a suspended in-memory choreography execution.

This has several consequences.

First, subject to those persistence and compatibility assumptions, the durable
workflow meaning can survive:

- deployment;
- process loss;
- browser suspension;
- load-balancer rerouting;
- reconnect;
- long periods without any participant online.

Second, later actors can acquire the relevant facts by authoritative
observation rather than requiring the original process to resume.

Third, the framework avoids making one runtime's memory an accidental source of
business truth.

*** A later choreography is not automatically the same execution

When durable state connects two periods of activity, the later protocol may be
a new execution with a new execution identity.

For example:

#+begin_example
E1:
    submit approval request
    -> command C1
    -> commit :pending
    -> terminate

E2:
    approver later acts
    -> command C2
    -> commit :approved
    -> terminate
#+end_example

The business process is continuous in domain meaning, but E1 and E2 need not be
one persistent protocol execution. E2 must establish its own authenticated
principal, current model eligibility, required knowledge, and applicable plan.
The existence of E1's durable result does not carry E1's transient authority or
resource ownership into E2.

This distinction avoids conflating:

- semantic command identity;
- protocol execution identity;
- durable domain identity;
- workflow/business identity.

Where an application truly requires a durable suspended execution, that feature
must define its own:

- persistence;
- ownership;
- versioning;
- migration;
- replay;
- resource cleanup;
- liveness;
- recovery semantics.

It is not silently inherited from ordinary Choreo.

*** Durable model state is the normal rendezvous point

For long workflows, authoritative state provides a stable rendezvous point
between actors who were never concurrently connected.

A later actor may know what happened because authority establishes:

#+begin_example
:approval/pending
:approval/approved
:request/claimed
:request/completed
#+end_example

not because it receives a packet from the actor who caused that state.

This follows [[#req-authoritative-observation][Part II's authoritative observation
model]]: observation is a first-class knowledge path.

Durable pending state is not itself a progress guarantee. The application must
provide the declared opportunity for an eligible actor to discover and act on
it, or state the environmental premises on which progress depends. Where loss
of a required participant can strand the workflow, the runtime-condition and
recovery requirements from Part I apply. Any lease, reassignment, cancellation,
or other successor policy remains model-owned.

*** Deployment compatibility remains explicit

A durable business fact may outlive the executable plan that originally
produced it.

That is normal.

What cannot be assumed is that a suspended *runtime execution* from an older
deployment remains compatible forever.

The later stale-plan/recovery design therefore treats durable model truth and
runtime-plan compatibility as separate dimensions.

A deployment may invalidate continuation of an old projection while leaving the
authoritative business state entirely valid. If a deployment also changes the
meaning or representation of persisted domain state, the model needs its own
compatible interpretation or migration. Restarting a current browser plan does
not perform that domain migration by implication.

* Part IV — Authoritative operations and progression
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: part-04-authoritative-operations-and-progression
:END:

This part defines the public model boundary, FX realization, atomic transitions,
scoped authoritative acquisition, and semantic command identity. It separates
authoritative reality from propagation status and participant knowledge. The
concrete browser coordinator follows in Part VI; whole-application capability
and acquisition closure follow in Part VII.

** Model integration
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-model-public-boundary
:END:

HumanHelp models remain the source of domain truth.

For a model such as Request:

#+begin_example
model/request/
  domain.clj
  schema.clj
  graph.clj
  fx.clj
  core.clj
  choreo.clj
#+end_example

The responsibilities are:

- =domain.clj= -- Request semantic rules and pure transitions;
- =schema.clj= -- Request persistence/value schemas;
- =graph.clj= -- Request graph declarations;
- =fx.clj= -- authoritative effect planning and atomic persistence realization;
- =core.clj= -- the narrow public semantic interface of the Request model;
- =choreo.clj= -- higher-level distributed programs built exclusively from
  public model interfaces.

The dependency direction is an architectural commitment:

#+begin_example
domain / schema / graph / fx
             |
             v
          core
             ^
             |
          choreo
#+end_example

=request.choreo= sits *above* =request.core=. It is not an implementation detail
behind =request.core= and =request.core= does not facade it.

A model choreography is therefore a higher-level distributed program written
against public model semantics.

=request.choreo= must not bypass the public model boundary and reach into
Request domain, graph, schema, or FX internals.

For example:

#+begin_src clojure
(ns net.humanhelp.site.model.request.choreo
  (:require
   [gesso.choreo.core :as choreo]
   [net.humanhelp.site.model.request.core :as request]))
#+end_src

and an authoritative choreography step calls something conceptually like:

#+begin_src clojure
(choreo/at :request-authority
  (request/claim ...))
#+end_src

not:

#+begin_src clojure
(request.fx/claim ...)
#+end_src

Cross-model dependencies follow the same rule. If a Request choreography needs
Membership or Organization semantics, it may require their public =core=
namespaces, never their internals:

#+begin_example
request.choreo
   |
   +--> request.core
   +--> membership.core
   +--> organization.core
#+end_example

The choreography may know facts that the semantic model API does not need to
know, including:

- roles;
- runtime actors;
- principals;
- communication;
- distributed knowledge;
- provenance;
- agreement;
- command identity;
- observed authoritative basis;
- provisional execution;
- settlement;
- timeout/retry behavior;
- convergence;
- projection compatibility/version.

Those concerns must not flow downward into the model merely because the model
has a choreography namespace. Some information, such as a semantic command
identity or an expected domain version, may independently belong in the public
model contract when durable deduplication or domain preconditions require it.
That does not make browser execution state or settlement transport a model
concern. The placement test below determines the owner of the meaning.

A useful placement test is:

#+begin_quote
Would this rule still matter if the public model operation were called
synchronously from one JVM with no browser, network, projection, or second
actor?
#+end_quote

If yes, it normally belongs in the model semantics below =core=.

If no because it concerns communication, distributed knowledge, optimism,
settlement, retry, projection compatibility, or convergence, it is a candidate
for =choreo.clj=.

A choreography involving one model's semantic operation but multiple runtime
actors may live in that model's =choreo.clj=.

A choreography whose meaning genuinely spans several peer models should live in
a higher-level flow/choreography namespace rather than turning one model into an
aggregation point.

For example, a future onboarding workflow that coordinates Invitation,
Membership, Organization, and external provisioning should not be forced into
=invitation.choreo= merely because Invitation happens to be the first step.

*** Model namespaces export semantic interfaces, not implementation privilege

The public =core= namespace is the semantic API boundary of a model.

A higher layer may rely on what that interface declares, but not on arbitrary
knowledge of the implementation beneath it.

For a Request model, a public operation contract may expose:

- semantic operation identity;
- typed inputs;
- typed outcomes;
- preconditions represented as public semantic commitments;
- authority requirements;
- atomicity guarantees;
- model-defined failure classes;
- stable facts/projections that higher layers may consume.

It should not require Choreo to know:

- XTDB transaction forms;
- internal graph query structure;
- implementation-only helper vars;
- private schema storage details;
- private FX staging data.

This is the boundary that allows the authoritative model to remain usable
without Choreo and allows Choreo to coordinate the model without becoming its
implementation.

Within one trusted Clojure process, a sufficiently determined application author
may still import lower-level namespaces, obtain a transaction handle, or write
to the database outside these interfaces.  Gesso does not claim to sandbox such
trusted host code.  The design goal inside that boundary is instead that normal
cooperating application code receives narrow semantic interfaces, accidental
bypass is difficult, and deliberate departure from the supported path is
conspicuous and outside the assurance claim that depended on the boundary.

Where practical, ordinary web/view/choreography code should not need a raw
authoritative mutation capability merely to perform a declared model operation.
This reduces accidental invariant bypass without pretending that Clojure records,
namespace privacy, or nominal wrappers constitute a security boundary against
the application author.

*** Semantic dependency is not necessarily namespace load order

The conceptual dependency:

#+begin_example
schema/domain/relationships/rules
        |
        v
FX / model operation contract
        |
        v
core
        |
        v
choreo
#+end_example

describes which semantic commitments may justify later reasoning.

It does not require the final source tree to mirror this diagram mechanically,
nor does it mean every namespace participates in the later proof system.

What matters is that a dependent semantic layer cannot claim a guarantee that
its dependencies neither establish nor explicitly assume.

*** Business rules remain model semantics

A rule belongs below =core= when its meaning would still matter in a synchronous
single-JVM call with no browser or distributed protocol.

Examples include:

- whether a Request may be claimed;
- whether Membership is active;
- whether an Organization permits an operation;
- what state transition follows from a valid command;
- what facts must remain true at authoritative commit.

Choreo may rely on the public result of those rules, but must not duplicate
them merely because a browser operation depends on them.

This prevents two dangerous architectures:

#+begin_example
model says one thing
view/choreo reimplements "close enough" version
#+end_example

and:

#+begin_example
core is only a facade
real business semantics secretly live in choreo
#+end_example

Neither is compatible with the intended end state.

*** Well-formed business rules and correct business policy are different

The model layer should eventually make malformed rule expressions rejectable
where their semantic types are known.

For example, if =request.status= is a closed enum, an ordered numeric comparison
against it is malformed and should not become a valid certified rule.

This stronger rule-language machinery is specified later in the compositional
proof part.

The architectural boundary established here is simpler:

- domain/model code owns business meaning;
- the semantic interface exposes only checked/declared commitments;
- Choreo consumes those commitments;
- Choreo does not decide whether product management chose the right policy.

*** Cross-model semantics do not imply cross-model leakage

A Request operation may depend on public facts from Membership and Organization.

That does not authorize =request.choreo= or =request.core= to reach through
those models into their internal FX/schema/graph implementation.

The dependency remains through public semantic interfaces.

Where several model facts must participate in one atomic transition, the
authoritative operation may compose the necessary model-level transaction work
behind the public operation contract.  API boundaries do not require separate
transactions.

The [[#req-atomic-authoritative-transition][atomic authoritative transition contract]]
below specifies this distinction in detail.

*** Higher-level cross-model choreography should be named at the right level

A distributed program whose meaning genuinely spans several peer models should
live in a higher-level flow/choreography namespace.

For example:

#+begin_example
onboarding.choreo
    |
    +--> invitation.core
    +--> membership.core
    +--> organization.core
    +--> provisioning.core
#+end_example

is preferable to making =invitation.choreo= own the entire workflow merely
because Invitation happens to be the first model touched.

The namespace location should reflect semantic ownership, not incidental
chronology.

*** HumanHelp is a reference consumer, not a source of generic Gesso semantics

HumanHelp may reveal missing generic abstractions.

When it does, the design test is:

#+begin_quote
Is this missing concept intrinsic to distributed semantic correctness for
Gesso applications generally, or is it HumanHelp business policy?
#+end_quote

Generic examples include:

- operation capability;
- knowledge/provenance requirement;
- transport requirement;
- server capability;
- acquisition requirement;
- progression contract;
- application-closure edge.

HumanHelp-specific examples include:

- who may claim a particular Request;
- Organization policy;
- Membership semantics;
- Request lifecycle business rules.

The former may justify additions to Gesso.  The latter remain in HumanHelp
models.

#+LATEX: \newpage
** Relationship between core, FX, and choreography

FX and Choreo do not merge into one execution mechanism.

They operate at different correctness scopes.

FX is responsible for realizing an authoritative model transition, usually
within one atomic transaction boundary.

Choreo is responsible for distributed coordination around use of that public
authoritative operation.

Conceptually:

#+begin_example
request.choreo/claim
        |
        | command, knowledge, observed basis, provisional state
        v
   authoritative step
        |
        v
request.core/claim
        |
        v
request.fx
        |
        | atomic XTDB transaction
        v
 authoritative result
        |
        v
request.choreo continues or terminates
        |
        | settlement / Live propagation / later authoritative observation
        v
actors learn result
#+end_example

There is one semantic operation identity, for example =:request/claim=, but the
public semantic operation and its higher-level distributed realization are not
the same function and need not have the same type.

Roughly:

#+begin_example
authoritative semantic operation

ClaimCommand x AuthoritativeWorld
        ->
ClaimResult


distributed choreographic operation

HelperKnowledge
        ->
Confirmed
| Reconciled
| Rejected
| LocallyRetiredWithOutcomeUnknown
| AnotherDeclaredProtocolOutcome
#+end_example

These alternatives are illustrative, not a required public enum. An execution
failure and an authoritative rejection are different outcomes; the former may
leave the command's disposition unknown to the participant.

The choreography should not invent a second, competing meaning for
=:request/claim=. Its authoritative step delegates to =request.core/claim=.

Likewise, =request.core= must not require or facade =request.choreo= merely to
present a single namespace to callers. The layering is intentionally public:

request.core: public Request semantics

request.choreo: public distributed programs built from Request semantics

Views and higher-level application code may consume prepared or bound
choreographic capabilities without weakening this dependency direction.

Choreo may rely on an authoritative operation's declared contract -- input and
result schemas, authority, atomicity, or other public metadata -- but it must not
understand or reproduce the model's XTDB writes.

This preserves an important independence property: =request.core/claim= remains
meaningful and testable from a REPL, batch job, test, server endpoint, or other
non-choreographic caller.

*** FX realizes model semantics; it does not define distributed truth

FX may perform multi-step computation, query authoritative state, prepare
transaction data, validate commit-time conditions, and publish an authoritative
result.

Those steps belong to realization of a model operation.

Choreo observes the public semantic boundary:

#+begin_example
authoritative operation invoked
        |
        v
declared semantic outcome
#+end_example

rather than treating every internal FX step as a distributed choreography
state.

This preserves a small and meaningful distributed protocol while allowing the
authoritative implementation to remain sophisticated.

*** One operation identity may span several implementation layers

The same semantic operation identity may be referenced by:

- model =core=;
- Choreo;
- UI operation capability;
- server operation registry;
- transport binding;
- diagnostics.

That does not mean all those layers are the same object.

For example:

#+begin_example
:request/claim
    |
    +--> request.core/claim
    |
    +--> request.choreo/claim
    |
    +--> bound UI capability
    |
    +--> trusted server realization
#+end_example

Each layer adds a different kind of structure around one semantic operation.

Later whole-application preflight must verify that independently realized edges
actually correspond.  It must not assume correspondence merely because names
look similar.
#+LATEX: \newpage
*** No semantic backchannel from Choreo into model internals

The following dependency is forbidden:

#+begin_example
request.choreo
    -> request.fx/private-prepare
    -> request.graph/private-query
#+end_example

even if it appears convenient.

Such a path lets distributed coordination depend on implementation facts that
the public model interface does not promise.

It also makes later proof/certification misleading because the apparent public
operation contract no longer captures the real dependencies.

The authoritative operation may internally use those facilities.  Choreo may
not.

*** Core remains meaningful without Choreo

A public model operation must remain useful in contexts such as:

- REPL invocation;
- tests;
- batch jobs;
- administrative tools;
- server APIs that do not need a distributed choreography;
- future application flows using a different higher-level protocol.

This is a deliberate independence property, not merely a convenience for
testing.

If =request.core/claim= only makes sense when called through
=request.choreo/claim=, business semantics have leaked upward.

Independent use does not mean bypassing required authority. A REPL, batch job,
or administrative caller must supply the trusted premises required by the same
public model contract. Removing the browser protocol must not remove current
authorization, transaction guards, or invariant enforcement.

*** Choreo adds distributed obligations that core should not own

Conversely, =request.core= should not need to understand:

- browser execution-instance identity and resource ownership;
- communication topology;
- projected branch knowledge;
- provisional DOM state;
- settlement transport;
- reconnect;
- continuity;
- stale projected-plan recovery.

Those are higher-level distributed/runtime concerns.

The two layers meet at an explicit authoritative operation contract.

*** Public operation metadata must be sufficient but minimal

Choreo may need public metadata describing an authoritative operation, such as:

- semantic identity;
- typed command/input;
- typed outcome/result;
- required authority;
- atomicity class;
- declared externally visible effects;
- model-defined reconciliation outcomes;
- stable facts that may be learned from the result.

This metadata should be rich enough for distributed reasoning without exposing
the operation's persistence implementation.

A useful test is:

#+begin_quote
Could a correct alternative implementation of this model operation satisfy the
same public semantic contract without Choreo changing?

If the answer is no only because Choreo depends on private implementation
details, the boundary is wrong.
#+end_quote


*** Model and choreography boundary invariant

At the end of this language/model layer, the unified design requires the
following relationship:

#+begin_quote
The choreography is the global specification of distributed control flow.
Projection produces role-local behavior justified by each role's knowledge.
Model =core= operations remain the public owners of authoritative business
semantics, FX realizes those operations below that boundary, and Choreo
coordinates their distributed use above it.  No layer gains stronger semantic
facts merely by crossing a representation, namespace, transport, or runtime
boundary.
#+end_quote


#+LATEX: \newpage

** Atomic authoritative transitions
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-atomic-authoritative-transition
:END:

If moving authoritative domain state A to B must be atomic, there must never be
an authoritative A-half state.

The computation deciding B may be multi-step. Establishment of B within the
declared authoritative transaction scope is one atomic step. Publication of an
invalidation or notification is a separate after-commit effect.

Conceptually:

#+begin_example
read authoritative facts
        |
derive / validate / decide
        |
prepare proposed transition
        |
identify commit-time guards
        |
one atomic transaction
        |
B
#+end_example

The important race is between evaluation and commit.

For example:

#+begin_example
read:
    Request R is unclaimed
    Membership U is active
    Organization permits the operation

decide:
    claim is allowed

             another transaction changes a relevant fact

commit:
    ???
#+end_example

An unconditional write at the end would be unsafe. The authoritative operation
must protect the facts whose incompatible change would invalidate the prepared
decision.

Semantically:

#+begin_example
read relevant authoritative facts
             |
             v
derive proposed transition
             |
             v
transaction:
    assert required preconditions still hold
    +
    apply every authoritative write in the transition
             |
          success?
          /     \
        yes      no
        |         |
        B      no partial B
                  |
                  v
            re-evaluate or
            return a semantic outcome
#+end_example

The exact XTDB mechanism used for commit guards is an implementation question for
the model/FX layer. The choreography language should express the authoritative
commit boundary and rely on the public model contract rather than reproducing
those guards itself.

Not every value read while computing a response must become a transaction guard.
Only facts whose incompatible change could invalidate the semantic transition
need protection.

Model boundaries are API boundaries, not necessarily transaction boundaries.

A Request operation may depend on facts exposed by Request, Membership, and
Organization public interfaces. If several authoritative writes collectively
constitute one invariant, they must be composed into one transaction rather than
exposed through several independently committing model calls.

The operation should conceptually distinguish:

- preparation;
- atomic commit;
- after-commit effects.

For example:

#+begin_src clojure
{:commit
 {:guards [...]
  :writes [...]}

 :after-commit
 [[:live/invalidate ...]
  [:notification/send ...]]}
#+end_src

The exact FX syntax may differ.

A failure established before submission of the proposed commit, or a confirmed
transaction rejection, establishes that this attempt did not commit its proposed
transition. It does not establish that the whole authoritative world remains at
A: other concurrent operations may have changed it.

Loss of contact after commit submission is different. If the trusted caller
cannot establish whether the transaction committed, it must preserve an unknown
commit outcome and use the declared resolution contract. An exception or timeout
does not by itself locate the failure before the commit boundary.

Once the authoritative commit succeeds, this transition established B even if
later publication, notification, SSE delivery, settlement delivery, or browser
rendering fails. Subsequent authoritative operations may advance the state
again; evidence of this commit is not a promise that B remains current forever.

Post-commit failure must therefore never be reported as though the authoritative
mutation itself had failed. Post-commit work requires its own delivery,
idempotency, retry, or convergence semantics.

If an operation spans an external system that cannot participate in the same
atomic transaction, any externally observable intermediate state must itself be
modeled as a valid domain state.

For example:

#+begin_example
:pending-provisioning
:awaiting-external-activation
#+end_example

The system must not hide an externally real partial state and merely hope nobody
observes it.

*** Authoritative transition contract

An authoritative operation exposes one semantic transition contract even when
its implementation contains several computational phases.

The contract must distinguish at least:

#+begin_example
prepare
    read and derive candidate transition

commit
    atomically establish the authoritative transition

after-commit
    publish, invalidate, notify, settle, or otherwise propagate consequences
#+end_example

Only the commit boundary changes authoritative domain truth.

This distinction is not merely operational bookkeeping.  It determines what
failure means.

When non-incorporation of this attempt is established:

#+begin_example
failure before commit submission, or confirmed transaction rejection
    -> this attempt did not establish its proposed authoritative transition
#+end_example

When submission occurred but the result is unavailable:

#+begin_example
commit request submitted
    -> acknowledgement/result lost
    -> commit outcome unknown to this participant
    -> resolve or retry only under the declared command/transaction contract
#+end_example

After commit:

#+begin_example
publication failure
notification failure
settlement-delivery failure
Live-delivery failure
browser-render failure
    -> authoritative transition DID occur
    -> propagation/recovery problem remains
#+end_example

A caller must never be told "the mutation failed" merely because a later
delivery mechanism failed after the authoritative transaction committed.

*** Preparation may be rich without weakening atomic publication

The computation that prepares an authoritative transition may involve:

- several model reads;
- deterministic derivations;
- relationship checks;
- current authorization/policy evaluation;
- construction of multiple writes;
- generation of command/result metadata;
- identification of commit-time guards.

There is no requirement that all of this logic fit into one tiny function.

The requirement is instead that any facts whose incompatible change would make
the prepared transition invalid are protected at commit.

Conceptually:

#+begin_example
authoritative read set
        |
        v
derive candidate transition
        |
        v
identify semantic dependencies that must remain compatible
        |
        v
atomic commit:
    guards + complete authoritative write set
#+end_example

The model must not confuse "was consulted during preparation" with "must be
guarded at commit." Guard the facts and predicates whose incompatible change
can invalidate the semantic transition.

Those predicates may concern absence, uniqueness, aggregate constraints, or
relationship membership rather than just rows returned by an earlier read.
Checking only previously read entity versions is insufficient if a concurrent
insertion or relationship change can violate the invariant. The model/FX
implementation must identify how its chosen transaction mechanism protects each
relevant predicate under the actual database contract.

Preparation also needs observations compatible with the decision being made.
Separate reads do not constitute a common snapshot merely because they were
performed close together. The operation must establish the snapshot or other
compatibility relation required by its invariant and preserve the required
premises through commit.

*** API boundaries do not force transaction boundaries

A public operation may depend upon facts owned by several models while still
forming one authoritative transaction.

For example, Request claim may require public semantic information from:

#+begin_example
Request
Membership
Organization
#+end_example

If the resulting authoritative change is one domain invariant, the
implementation may compose the necessary transaction work behind the public
Request operation contract.

The design rejects this inference:

#+begin_example
different model APIs
    => separate independent commits
#+end_example

Model APIs organize semantic ownership. Atomicity is determined by the domain
transition and the scope supported by the authoritative system. Composition must
retain every participating model's required constraints; combining write forms
alone does not establish that their contracts have been satisfied.

If the selected stores cannot realize the required joint atomic transition, the
operation cannot be admitted as providing that atomicity guarantee. It needs a
different realization or an explicitly modeled multi-step domain protocol.

*** External systems require explicit valid intermediate states

When an external system cannot participate in the authoritative transaction,
Gesso must not pretend the overall world changes atomically.

If external provisioning is observable between local commit and external
success, that intermediate situation must be represented as a valid domain
state, for example:

#+begin_example
:pending-provisioning
:provisioning-failed
:awaiting-external-activation
#+end_example

The model may then define subsequent transitions that resolve that state.
Compensation, where supported, is a new authoritative transition with its own
conditions and failure semantics. It does not erase the fact that an earlier
external effect occurred or guarantee that every external consequence can be
undone.

What is forbidden is an externally real partial transition whose existence is
hidden merely because the application wished the operation were atomic.

*** FX value-flow architecture

The Biff FX substrate used by Gesso must preserve a sharp distinction among:

- ambient execution context;
- machine invocation arguments;
- previous-state output.

The intended state-machine discipline is conceptually:

#+begin_src clojure
:start
(fn [ctx operation-input]
  ...)

:next
(fn [ctx previous-output]
  ...)
#+end_src

Ordinary state output must not become ambient context merely because an earlier
state returned it.

This means:

#+begin_example
ctx
    stable/ambient capabilities and environment

operation-input
    explicit value supplied when the machine is invoked

previous-output
    explicit value produced by the preceding state
#+end_example

This distinction is an architectural invariant, not a temporary compatibility
constraint.

It aligns FX execution with the semantic boundaries established in Parts I–III:

- ambient authority/capability remains ambient;
- operation input remains explicit;
- intermediate computation remains explicit state flow;
- a later state cannot accidentally acquire authority or data merely because
  some earlier state placed a key into an accumulated context map.

*** Ambient context is capability-bearing, not a hidden data bus

Examples of values that may legitimately live in ambient context include:

- authenticated request/server environment;
- database/runtime handles;
- clock capability;
- randomness/seed capability;
- transaction execution facilities;
- trusted configuration;
- other explicitly supplied execution capabilities.

Examples of values that should normally move through explicit machine state
instead include:

- operation input;
- queried facts produced by an earlier state;
- a prepared model command;
- a transaction plan;
- a previous semantic outcome;
- derived values needed by the next machine state.

This keeps state-machine meaning inspectable.

A machine that requires a value should receive it through a declared path rather
than relying on accidental context accumulation.

An available clock or transaction facility is a capability; a particular sampled
time or transaction outcome is an explicit value. Merely placing a value in
=ctx= does not establish trust. The trusted invocation boundary owns the origin
and permitted use of capability-bearing context, and untrusted input must not
overwrite it through an ordinary map merge.

Required capabilities and their supplied realizations must be inspectable at
construction. A structurally missing required clock, principal binding, or
transaction facility must fail admission before first invocation when the
absence is already knowable. Concrete authentication, availability, and policy
remain runtime premises.

*** FX state boundaries preserve semantic ownership

An FX state may compute or realize part of a public model operation, but passing
through an FX state does not upgrade provenance or authority.

For example:

#+begin_example
client supplied argument
    -> validated operation input
    -> FX state
#+end_example

does not make the argument authoritative merely because trusted server code
handled it.

Likewise:

#+begin_example
query result
    -> machine state output
#+end_example

does not automatically become a choreography fact known by some remote actor.

Semantic provenance still comes from the appropriate model, authority,
knowledge, or projection rule.

*** Ordered effects are explicit semantics

Where effect ordering matters, the FX representation must express ordering
explicitly rather than relying on incidental collection interpretation.

The supported ordered-effect mechanism is part of the execution contract. It
must distinguish sequencing from successful completion, and define what happens
to later effects when an earlier one fails. Ordering by itself supplies neither
atomicity across effects nor durable retry after process loss.

A sequence such as:

#+begin_example
commit
then publish invalidation
then enqueue notification
#+end_example

must have a representation whose ordering semantics are unambiguous to the FX
runtime.

A vector that is merely an effect's value must not be confused with a sequence
of effects.

This matters because post-commit correctness often depends on distinguishing:

#+begin_example
what must occur atomically
what must occur afterward
what may retry independently
#+end_example

*** Graph/multistate integrations obey the same value-flow rule

Any multistate graph/resolver facility built on the same FX execution model must
respect the same separation:

#+begin_example
ambient ctx
invocation/resolver input
previous state output
#+end_example

Later states may not silently recover original or intermediate values from
ambient context unless those values are intentionally part of that context.

The exact graph API is subordinate to the invariant.

*** Operation contracts describe effects without exposing implementation details

A public model operation may expose semantic information such as:

- typed inputs;
- typed outcomes;
- authority requirements;
- atomicity;
- declared externally visible effects;
- preconditions;
- postconditions;
- model-defined failure classes.

It should not require Choreo to understand its internal FX state graph.

The later compositional-proof architecture may certify that an FX/operation
implementation satisfies such a contract.  The contract boundary exists
independently of that stronger proof machinery.

*** Post-commit delivery debt is explicit

Once authoritative commit is established, outstanding propagation work is
delivery debt. It does not undo that commit.

A participant lacking adequate evidence may nevertheless remain uncertain about
its command. Authoritative disposition, propagation status, and each
participant's knowledge are separate dimensions. The
[[#req-command-participant-uncertainty][command uncertainty contract]] below applies
even when another participant already knows that the commit succeeded.

Examples include:

- Live invalidation not yet published;
- notification not yet delivered;
- settlement response lost;
- browser disconnected before observing the result;
- downstream cache not yet refreshed.

The system should model or diagnose this state honestly.

Recovery may use:

- retry;
- idempotent publication;
- later authoritative reread;
- advisory invalidation;
- reconciliation by command identity;
- another application-specific convergence mechanism.

It must never "roll back" the truth conceptually merely because an observer did
not learn it immediately. An authoritative reread can recover current state
without necessarily establishing which command produced it.

**** Publication and recovery contract
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-post-commit-recovery
:END:

Every claimed commit-to-observation guarantee must identify the propagation and
recovery mechanism and its environmental premises. In particular, it must
address process loss after commit but before publication, repeated publication,
and loss of the original settlement where those failures are in scope.

A connected observer that misses publication may have no evidence that anything
changed. The design must not infer eventual notification merely from the
existence of an SSE connection or from successful commit. The supported
realization may provide durable publication intent, reconciliation on reconnect,
periodic acquisition, explicit user Refresh, or another declared path. Each
supports a different claim and must be described accordingly.

If recovery depends on user Refresh, convergence is conditional on that action.
If automatic eventual publication is claimed, the mechanism must retain or
reconstruct the necessary work across the failures covered by that claim.
Gesso does not require one universal outbox or durable workflow engine.

Retrying propagation must not re-execute the authoritative mutation unless the
command's retry contract explicitly permits that and establishes its safety.
The operational acceptance must exercise the selected recovery path, including
the commit-before-publication failure window, against the actual production
realization.

** Authoritative basis and XTDB

XTDB supplies the authoritative database progression/consistency coordinate.

Gesso is intentionally allowed to assume XTDB for its primary persistent
application authority. Database/backend independence is not a current design
goal when pursuing it would materially increase complexity or weaken
correctness.

The semantic vocabulary should still avoid accidental coupling to a particular
XTDB Java class or serialization format, and authority remains conceptually
broader than one database because external systems may establish their own
facts.

Gesso should reuse XTDB's supported consistency mechanisms rather than inventing
a second generic global revision system.

The generic semantic layer distinguishes at least three related categories even
when their concrete representation contains the same XTDB token:

- =AuthoritativeBasis= -- a basis established by a trusted authoritative
  acquisition or trusted runtime boundary;
- =ObservedBasisClaim= -- an adversarial participant's statement about the basis
  from which it claims to have acted;
- =ProgressionRequirement= -- a requirement that a future authoritative
  acquisition must satisfy for some declared scope.

These names describe meanings, not a mandate for three public wrapper classes.
The implementation may reuse one token representation while preventing the
supported APIs from treating the categories as interchangeable without the
required derivation/admission step.

A browser representation may be known to have been derived from a read
consistent through some authoritative coordinate X17.

X17 does *not* mean:

- Request revision 17;
- the seventeenth state of the application;
- that Request changed seventeen times;
- that the database has not advanced since the browser rendered;
- that every actor knows the same facts.

When established by trusted acquisition, it records a consistency coordinate
for that observation's declared scope. Carried back by an untrusted browser, the
same bytes are only an observed-basis claim unless stronger evidence is
established. Neither form alone states that the actor acquired the whole
authoritative world.

A command may carry protocol data such as:

#+begin_example
:command-id
:observed-basis     ; an ObservedBasisClaim at the untrusted boundary
