Composition is where independent certified artifacts become one larger trusted
semantic construction.

The default rule is strict:

#+begin_quote
A dependent stage accepts only dependencies whose certified semantic interfaces
satisfy the exact assumptions and identity requirements of the dependent
construction.
#+end_quote

A dependent stage must reject immediately if:

- a required semantic dependency is missing;
- a supplied dependency has the wrong certificate kind;
- semantic identities do not match the dependency named in the certificate;
- the required semantic-checker or proof-kernel version is unsupported;
- an upstream guarantee needed by the dependent stage is absent;
- an assumption is supplied with an incompatible proposition/type;
- two dependencies export incompatible definitions of the same semantic
  identity;
- a proof obligation remains unresolved;
- a supposedly closed rule/message/variant set contains an undeclared member;
- a dependency graph contains an unsupported cycle;
- a stale certificate is paired with a changed semantic interface;
- a certificate claims a theorem not derivable under its recorded premises.

A failure at =request.rules= means no =VerifiedRequestRules= exists.

Therefore:

#+begin_example
no VerifiedRequestRules
  -> no VerifiedRequestOperationRegistry
  -> no VerifiedRequestChoreography
  -> no VerifiedProjection
  -> no certified ExecutablePlan
#+end_example

A production build should not "try anyway."

*** Goals

Semantic identity must allow:

- stale proof detection;
- deterministic caching;
- dependency mismatch rejection;
- reproducible build diagnostics;
- proof DAG inspection.

It must avoid invalidating the world for irrelevant source edits.

*** Normalize before fingerprinting

Fingerprint normalized semantic terms, not source text.

For example, a rule's semantic identity may include:

- normalized operator tree;
- resolved nominal types;
- resolved semantic field ids;
- declared assumptions;
- declared guarantees.

It should exclude:

- whitespace;
- comments;
- local source formatting;
- unrelated helper implementation;
- diagnostic-only source spans.

Source locations remain attached separately for diagnostics.

*** Dependency identities are transitive evidence, not transitive hashes alone

A certificate records direct certified dependency identities.

The root proof DAG can reconstruct the transitive dependency closure.

Do not mistake a Merkle-like dependency hash for proof validity. The checker
still validates certificate structure and derivations.

*** Compatibility rather than equality where justified

Not every downstream dependency requires byte-identical semantics.

Some certified interfaces may admit an explicit compatibility relation such as:

#+begin_example
new interface provides every guarantee required by old dependent
and does not invalidate a relied-upon closed-world assumption
#+end_example

Do not implement semantic subtyping/compatibility speculatively. Begin with exact
semantic identity where correctness is clearer, then generalize only for a
concrete evolution problem.

*** Identity evidence is not proof evidence

Semantic identity answers:

#+begin_quote
Is this the normalized semantic interface whose identity the dependent artifact
records?
#+end_quote

It does not answer:

#+begin_quote
Was that interface correctly certified?
#+end_quote

Both checks matter.

A copied semantic ID on a forged raw value is not a certificate.

A valid certificate for the wrong semantic dependency is not compatible merely
because the artifact class matches.

*** Staleness propagates through dependency edges

Suppose:

#+begin_example
organization.domain O1
    |
claim-rules R1 depends on O1
    |
claim-operation C1 depends on R1
    |
claim-choreo H1 depends on C1
    |
projection P1 depends on H1
#+end_example

If the semantic interface becomes O2 and R1 relied on changed meaning, R1 no
longer composes.

The ordinary dependency chain stops there.

Later stages do not receive permission to keep using C1/H1/P1 simply because
their own serialized bytes have not changed.

*** Compatibility must be earned

Exact semantic identity is the safe initial rule.

An explicit compatibility relation may admit evolution such as:

#+begin_example
new interface
    provides every guarantee required by old dependent
    preserves all closed-world assumptions that dependent relied upon
#+end_example

but that compatibility relation is itself semantic machinery requiring checks.

"Looks backwards compatible" is not a supported proof rule.

*** Assumption propagation and discharge
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-certificate-assumption-propagation
:END:

Assumptions are scoped propositions, not just names. Their identity includes the
relevant subject, parameters, semantic context, and dependencies. Two equal
labels with different propositions cannot be merged. The report must make the
assumptions of each exported guarantee inspectable through the dependency DAG;
flattening a root list must not lose which claims depend on which premise.

A downstream derivation may discharge an assumption only by supplying an
accepted proof of the required proposition under a checked substitution and
compatible scope. Its replacement proof's own assumptions remain dependencies.
The original unresolved premise may disappear from the open-assumption list,
but the discharge evidence remains reconstructible. Renaming or copying the
premise into another certificate is not discharge.

Runtime authorization and authenticated-principal premises require the actual
invocation boundary; a static certificate that names a principal or capability
does not satisfy them for future requests. Environmental liveness premises
remain attached to the claims that use them. No certificate supplies fairness,
network recovery, or a user's eventual Refresh action simply by requiring it.

An artifact class must state which assumptions it permits and which obligations
must be checked rather than asserted. Unsupported syntax, missing dependencies,
and invalid proof rules cannot be excused by automatically converting failure
into an application assumption. An explicitly weaker artifact/profile is a
different result and cannot satisfy a stronger requested milestone.

The checker rejects detectable contradictions within its supported logic and
incompatible definitions of the same scoped proposition. It need not decide
arbitrary consistency of application policy or opaque trusted predicates.
A conditional theorem under externally inconsistent assumptions does not prove
a realizable usable application. Positive HumanHelp scenarios and independent
behavioral acceptance remain necessary to prevent vacuous completion claims.

*** Semantic reuse and implementation evidence

Exact semantic identity permits reuse only for claims that depend on that
semantic interface. Evidence about a concrete implementation, provider binding,
compiler, generated artifact, or browser integration must remain attached to
the corresponding implementation and admitted assembly-generation identities,
where relevant, and the environment scope.
An implementation change can preserve all downstream contract-level derivations
while requiring renewed implementation conformance or test evidence.

Caching must key admission on the normalized subject, dependencies, rule set,
checker semantics/version, certification profile, and relevant assumptions.
Source spans may change without semantic invalidation, but diagnostics must
still identify the source associated with the current construction. An old
accepted result cannot override an explicitly unsupported or withdrawn checker
profile in the current admission policy.

Admission of one immutable dependency closure must not race mutable lookups
that later substitute a different dependency. Construction either retains the
admitted closure or detects changes and rechecks before use. This follows
Part VII's admitted assembly-generation binding; it does not require one global lock or storage
implementation.


** Proof kernel and proof-producing elaboration
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-proof-kernel-boundary
:END:

The logical trusted base should remain much smaller than the compiler that
constructs semantic artifacts.

The end state should have a deliberately small trusted proof kernel.

The large compiler/elaborator is allowed to be complicated. The proof kernel
must remain small enough to inspect independently.

The canonical terminology from Part I distinguishes three responsibilities:

- The *executable semantic core* determines production transitions from executable
  rules, admitted state, and inputs/events, producing state and declared effects.
- The *proof kernel* accepts or rejects derivations under explicit inference
  rules and premises. It does not execute the application trajectory or supply
  runtime facts, ownership, or authorization.
- A *dedicated semantic checker* establishes its stated judgment through its
  own checking mechanism, such as structural closure or exact finite exploration.
  Its result retains the evidence class and trusted premises of that mechanism.

These components may share canonical semantic definitions, but sharing does not
merge their responsibilities or prove implementation conformance. The proof
kernel checks proposed derivations independently of the elaborator's claimed
success. The production executable semantic core remains necessary regardless
of the certification profile; introducing the later proof kernel does not create
a second runtime semantics.

This terminology does not prescribe a new namespace, expand the supported rule
language, or settle the judgment-calculus definitions deferred by this part.

Conceptually:

#+begin_example
raw application syntax
        |
        v
large elaborator/compiler
        |
        | proposes terms + derivations
        v
small proof kernel
        |
        | accepts/rejects derivation
        v
certificate
#+end_example

A compiler bug that generates an invalid derivation must therefore fail at the
proof-kernel boundary. This guarantee alone does not cover omitted obligations,
incorrect source normalization, or incorrect executable lowering. Those require
their own checked correspondence or explicit trusted premises.

*** Candidate proof-kernel responsibilities

Keep the proof kernel small enough to audit.

Possible responsibilities:

- nominal type equality;
- closed enum/variant membership;
- rule-expression typing;
- presence/refinement entailment;
- knowledge entailment;
- provenance entailment;
- authority/capability entailment;
- conjunction/disjunction of propositions;
- declared deterministic derivation application;
- message contract matching;
- transition precondition/postcondition compatibility;
- certificate dependency matching;
- selected resource linearity/ownership rules;
- selected properties of the declared progression requirement-combination
  contract, without assuming every implementation supplies a universal join;
- selected projection proof obligations.

Some properties may remain separate model checkers at first if forcing them into
one proof kernel would obscure rather than reduce the trusted base.

*** Proof-kernel non-responsibilities

Do not put these in the proof kernel:

- arbitrary Clojure evaluation;
- DOM behavior;
- HTMX implementation;
- network scheduling;
- XTDB internals;
- arbitrary arithmetic theorem proving;
- general higher-order logic;
- arbitrary user predicates;
- automatic proof search over an unbounded domain;
- full dependent typing.

Existing verifier code should progressively move from:

#+begin_src clojure
{:valid? true}
#+end_src

toward producing explicit evidence.

For example:

#+begin_example
static goal:
  after admitted observation O of Request R at program point q,
  browser knows O's request.status value for R

required premises:
  trusted acquisition contract produces an observation of R
  O's scope includes R and its status field
  projection includes that field under the relevant branch
  browser receives and admits O through the declared event boundary
  provenance and any required basis/compatibility obligations hold

checked conclusion:
  Knows(browser, status-of(R, O), after(q))

not concluded:
  the browser has already received a future observation
  every Request or the whole board is current at O's basis
  observation alone authorizes a later mutation
#+end_example

The exact proof-term representation should remain ordinary immutable data.

The diagnostic renderer and external formalization bridge should be able to consume the same
proof vocabulary. The static derivation establishes a conditional transition
property. Concrete observation admission remains a runtime boundary under
[[#req-authoritative-observation]]; a proof sidecar cannot manufacture O.

*** Obligation coverage and executable correspondence

The accepting checker must validate a complete judgment for its artifact class,
not merely accept a bag of individually valid derivations. It must bind every
exported conclusion to the normalized subject and verify the required coverage:
for example, every relevant rule term, reachable transition, branch,
communication contract, and terminal/resource case in the claimed subset.
Unreachable-case exclusion itself needs the justification required by that
subset. Removing a troublesome branch from the proposed obligation list cannot
make the original choreography certifiable.

A checker can accomplish this by deriving required obligations itself, checking
a syntax-directed derivation covering the subject, or another explicit complete
coverage mechanism. The mechanism and any trusted generator remain named in
the assurance report; this document does not require one implementation.

There are distinct correspondence boundaries from author syntax to normalized
subject, from that subject to certified projection, and from projection to the
actual executable plan consumed by production. An accepted proof about a
silently changed subject is not proof about the author's original declaration.
Tests provide evidence for these bindings; only a stated checked preservation
argument permits a stronger correspondence claim.

The logical trusted base includes inference-rule semantics, checker code,
primitive semantics, and the representation/parser assumptions used in checking.
A small proof kernel reduces that surface; it does not prove its own implementation
sound merely by being small. Dedicated checkers add their own stated trusted
surface or supply evidence accepted by a smaller checker.

Checking failure, resource exhaustion, timeout, missing evidence, and unsupported
features yield distinct non-admitted results where relevant. They never mean
that an obligation was proved false, nor that it passed. Resource limits must
not turn incomplete search or exploration into successful certification.

*** Proof-kernel/elaborator separation is a fault-containment boundary

The elaborator may contain bugs.

The architecture is useful only if an elaborator bug that proposes an invalid
derivation can be rejected by the smaller checker.

Therefore mutation tests should deliberately construct:

- missing premises;
- wrong dependency references;
- invalid type conclusions;
- unjustified provenance strengthening;
- bogus authority entailment;
- malformed refinement derivations;
- fake rule tags;
- stale dependency identities;
- omitted obligations for a retained branch or term;
- a valid derivation for the wrong subject or program point;
- an unjustified assumption discharge or changed scoped proposition;
- a semantically different normalized term paired with the original declaration;
- an executable plan altered after valid projection certification.

The appropriate checker or correspondence boundary must reject each defect;
a proof kernel is not credited with checking an external binding it
does not inspect.

The proof kernel accepts or rejects the proof term independently of the elaborator's
claim that the term is valid.

*** Dedicated semantic checkers may coexist with the proof kernel

Not every property must immediately be forced into one universal logic.

A small exact transition-system checker may remain the right tool for:

- bounded fragment coordination;
- adapter ownership invariants;
- particular finite-state properties.

Such a result can still participate in the root assurance report with an honest
classification. A finite checker must record the modeled state dimensions,
initial states, transitions, property, and exploration scope. One active request
and one queued slot do not make retained metadata or physical retired requests
finite. A claim about the concrete coordinator requires the relevant sound
abstraction/refinement relation to [[#req-fragment-coordinator]].

The report distinguishes completed exact exploration, a discovered
counterexample, and incomplete exploration. It cannot equate running every
currently generated trace with exploring every behavior of the claimed model.

The goal is a smaller trusted story, not maximal unification for its own sake.

** Malformedness boundary and policy correctness

The proof architecture deliberately distinguishes malformed semantic
expressions from coherent but product-wrong policies.

Every negative downstream test should classify the defect.

Categories include:

- =:syntax=;
- =:unknown-semantic-reference=;
- =:type-mismatch=;
- =:invalid-cardinality=;
- =:missing-refinement=;
- =:closed-world-violation=;
- =:knowledge-not-established=;
- =:provenance-not-established=;
- =:authority-not-established=;
- =:capability-not-established=;
- =:relationship-mismatch=;
- =:operation-contract-mismatch=;
- =:continuation-mismatch=;
- =:message-contract-mismatch=;
- =:projection-obligation-failed=;
- =:resource-obligation-failed=;
- =:semantic-dependency-mismatch=;
- =:stale-certificate=;
- =:unsupported-certificate-version=;
- =:proof-invalid=;
- =:unresolved-obligation=.

A stable taxonomy is useful both for developer diagnostics and for proof/mutation
coverage accounting.

This architecture must preserve this boundary rigorously.

Accepted but potentially wrong business policy:

#+begin_src clojure
(rule adult?
  (> user.age 18))
#+end_src

where =user.age= is an integer.

Gesso cannot know whether the business intended “older than 18” or “at least
18.” The product owns that choice.

Rejected malformed rule:

#+begin_src clojure
(rule adult?
  (> user.email 18))
#+end_src

where =user.email= is an email/string-like nominal type.

Rejected valid rule at an invalid choreography point:

#+begin_src clojure
(rule approved?
  (= request.private-review :approved))
#+end_src

if the browser tries to branch on =approved?= before it has justified knowledge
of =request.private-review=.

The proof system checks coherence and admissibility, not product intent.

*** Distributed admissibility remains a second check

A rule may be intrinsically well typed yet unusable by a role at a particular
point.

Therefore the full judgment is conceptually:

#+begin_example
rule intrinsically valid?
        |
        v
required facts known here?
        |
required provenance available?
        |
required authority/trust justified?
        |
required optional refinements established?
        |
required authoritative basis/progression available?
        |
        v
rule admissible at this distributed program point
#+end_example

This separation prevents ordinary type correctness from being mistaken for
distributed knowledge correctness.

** Diagnostics as proof and repair output

A rejected composition is only useful if the system preserves enough semantic
context to explain the first failed obligation.

A failed proof composition should explain why the proof cannot be constructed.

Example:

#+begin_example
Gesso proof error: :type-mismatch

Rule:
  humanhelp.request/claimable?

Expression:
  (< request.status 10)

Operator:
  <

Required:
  Ordered<A> x Ordered<A> -> Boolean

Resolved operands:
  request.status : RequestStatus
  10             : Integer

RequestStatus has no declared ordering.

Primary source:
  request/domain/rules.clj:...

Proof chain stopped at:
  VerifiedRequestRules

Downstream artifacts not constructed:
  VerifiedRequestOperationRegistry
  VerifiedRequestChoreography
  VerifiedRequestProjection
  ExecutablePlan
#+end_example

Another:

#+begin_example
Gesso proof error: :semantic-dependency-mismatch

request.rules was certified against:
  request.schema semantic-id S17

current request.schema:
  semantic-id S18

Relevant semantic change:
  RequestStatus removed :claimed
  RequestStatus added :assigned

No stale request.rules certificate may be composed.
#+end_example

Another:

#+begin_example
Gesso proof error: :knowledge-not-established

Role:
  :helper-browser

Behavior diverges on:
  request.private-review

Rule itself:
  VERIFIED

Use at this choreography state:
  REJECTED

Available provenance:
  none

Required:
  knowledge of this field for this subject at this program point,
  with the provenance required by the branch's contract

Candidate establishment path (subject to authorization and scope):
  authoritative request projection at state ...
#+end_example

** Systematic downstream mutation corpus
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-certification-mutation-corpus
:END:

The mutation corpus is the adversarial executable specification of what malformed
downstream construction must not cross a certified boundary.

This should become a major acceptance suite.

Begin with several known-good certified programs.

Generate or hand-author semantic mutations one at a time and require classified
rejection at the first applicable boundary. A structurally valid browser request
containing an untrusted principal assertion may require runtime rejection rather
than construction-time rejection; acceptance names the actual enforcing boundary.

Each case records its valid baseline, the isolated change, affected proposition,
expected result, first enforcing boundary, stable diagnostic category, and
whether the prohibited artifact/effect was prevented. Re-run the positive
baseline through that same path: a checker that rejects every program does not
satisfy the acceptance contract.

*** Schema/domain mutations

- remove a referenced field;
- change a field's nominal type;
- change required to optional;
- change optional to required;
- remove an enum variant;
- add a closed-world variant where exhaustiveness matters;
- change relationship cardinality;
- change relationship endpoint type.

*** Rule mutations

- compare incompatible types;
- order an unordered type;
- access optional value without refinement;
- use unknown field;
- use unknown variant;
- omit a match branch;
- use a relationship predicate on the wrong model;
- reference an undeclared operation output.

*** Knowledge/provenance mutations

- remove communication;
- remove authoritative observation;
- give entry knowledge to the wrong role;
- use optional communicated data as definite knowledge;
- turn authoritative provenance into asserted provenance where stronger
  provenance is required;
- derive from an input the actor does not know;
- branch before discriminator knowledge exists.

*** Authority/security mutations

- browser supplies role;
- browser supplies principal;
- browser supplies authoritative provenance;
- browser supplies capability;
- browser requests undeclared operation;
- browser asks for protected observation/subscription without server
  authorization;
- operation contract requires authority absent from the actor projection.

*** Choreography/projection mutations

- make remote behavior depend on an unobservable branch;
- remove required correlation;
- overlap receive contracts;
- change continuation type;
- omit terminal path;
- leak resource on one branch;
- reuse a retired execution or resource-instance identity to claim current
  ownership;
- pair a projection with the wrong verified choreography identity.

*** Certificate mutations

- alter semantic-id;
- alter dependency id;
- alter guarantee without changing derivation;
- drop a premise;
- inject a fake proof rule;
- change checker version;
- compose certificate against stale dependency;
- create dependency cycle;
- replace certified value with same-shaped raw map;
- omit a required obligation while retaining its subject transition;
- keep a guarantee but alter its assumption scope;
- substitute an implementation or served artifact while retaining its old report;
- return a partial exploration result as a successful exact check.

The expected classification is specified before executing the mutant. A changed
schema need not be intrinsically malformed: the required rejection may be the
old dependent certificate composing against it. An unchanged-semantic edit or a
coherent product-wrong rule can be an intentional accepted control. Mutation
acceptance must not retroactively relabel an unexpected checker miss as an
application-truth exception.

Every completed mutant case must be either:

#+begin_example
REJECTED
with stable reason and proof location
#+end_example

or deliberately recorded as:

#+begin_example
ACCEPTED BY DESIGN
property belongs to application truth or an explicitly trusted boundary
#+end_example

There should be no ambiguous "probably okay" bucket. Infrastructure failure,
timeout, or an unsupported test mechanism is an incomplete test, not either
successful outcome above. An expected unsupported-feature rejection may pass
its explicit negative case; it does not demonstrate support for that feature.
A score over mutants is evidence about that corpus, not a soundness theorem.

** HumanHelp proof DAG and compositional pilot
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-humanhelp-certification-pilot
:END:

HumanHelp Request Claim is the first substantial application proof chain.

The graph should extend through the whole application rather than stopping at
the projected runtime artifact.

Conceptually:

#+begin_example
user.schema --------------------------+
                                      |
user.domain --------------------------+------+
                                             |
organization.schema -----------------+      |
                                      |      |
organization.domain -----------------+------+------+
                                                    |
request.schema -------------------------------------+
                                                    |
request.domain -------------------------------------+
                                                    |
request/user relationships -------------------------+
                                                    |
claim rules ----------------------------------------+
                                                    |
request claim operation contract -------------------+
                                                    |
request claim choreography -------------------------+
                         |                          |
                         v                          v
                 helper projection         authority projection
                         |                          |
                         +------------+-------------+
                                      |
                                      v
                              ExecutablePlan
                                      |
                                      v
                        HumanHelp ApplicationAssembly
                         /       |       |        \
                        v        v       v         v
                 browser      transport route   server realization
                 artifact                         |
                     \                             v
                      +------------------> authoritative model op
                                             |
                                             v
                                  settlement/progression
                                             |
                                             v
                                  authoritative acquisition
                                             |
                                             v
                                  root application report
#+end_example

The root report does not claim every edge is a theorem checked by one proof kernel.

It records the assurance class of each edge. The diagram mixes semantic
dependencies with explanatory execution flow; only certified justification
edges form the proof DAG. Repeated runtime acquisition or protocol loops do not
create permission for circular certificate justification.

An upstream semantic mismatch must nevertheless prevent downstream construction
when the downstream artifact depends on the changed commitment.

HumanHelp Request provides the first real downstream demonstration that
framework guarantees compose across application-owned semantic layers.

The pilot is not successful merely because a toy proof term works.

It should cover one meaningful production operation with real cross-model
dependencies.

Request Claim is the preferred conceptual target when its production contracts
are stable enough.

*** Pilot dependency chain

The root proof DAG should include, where the operation genuinely depends on
them:

- Request schema;
- User/Membership schema and domain commitments;
- Organization schema/domain commitments;
- Request domain refinements;
- relationship/graph facts;
- Claim rule set;
- Request Claim authoritative operation contract;
- Request Claim choreography;
- Helper projection;
- Request-authority projection;
- executable browser/server plan artifacts;
- HumanHelp application assembly realization;
- explicit application/trusted assumptions.

The chain must not invent dependencies solely to make the graph look impressive.

*** Upstream semantic breakage stops downstream construction

Suppose =organization.domain= changes a semantic guarantee relied upon by Claim
rules.

The desired result is:

#+begin_example
organization.domain semantic-id changes
    |
old claim-rules dependency no longer matches
    |
VerifiedClaimRules cannot be constructed
    |
Claim operation certificate cannot compose
    |
Claim choreography cannot certify
    |
projection cannot certify
    |
ExecutablePlan cannot certify
    |
HumanHelp application root cannot close
#+end_example

The system should fail at the first stage that has enough information to know
the dependency mismatch.

The browser must never be asked to discover it.

*** Application truth remains explicit

The Claim pilot will still contain HumanHelp-specific assumptions that Gesso
cannot derive from generic semantics.

For example, the application may assert a particular business policy relation.

That assertion is acceptable if:

- named;
- typed;
- attached to its source;
- propagated transitively into dependent certificates;
- visible in the root report.

It is not silently converted into a framework theorem.

*** Cross-model authority stays public-interface based

If Claim depends on Membership and Organization semantics, the proof DAG consumes
their certified public semantic interfaces.

It does not reach into their private FX/query implementation merely because that
would make proof extraction easier.

The proof architecture must reinforce, not erode, the model ownership boundary
from Part IV.

*** Pilot must include adversarial mutants

The HumanHelp pilot includes mutants such as:

- remove a required Request status variant;
- change Membership relationship cardinality;
- remove an Organization guarantee required by Claim;
- stale the Claim rule certificate;
- forge a same-shaped operation certificate;
- remove browser knowledge of a branch discriminator;
- give the browser asserted provenance where authoritative provenance is required;
- pair the projection with the wrong choreography identity;
- omit the application transport edge;
- pair the plan with a stale browser artifact.

Each mutant should fail at the earliest appropriate layer with a stable
classification.

*** Pilot root report

A successful pilot emits a report that answers at least:

- what Claim operation is certified;
- which model semantic interfaces it depends on;
- which assumptions remain application/trusted;
- what Choreo properties are proved/checked;
- what projections were derived;
- what executable plan was emitted;
- what whole-application edges are assembly-verified;
- what runtime premises remain;
- what trusted external assumptions remain;
- which normalized subject, checker profile, implementation bindings, and
  actual admitted assembly generation the evidence covers;
- which required operational claims are separately supported by real-browser
  and production-enforcement evidence;
- which certification obligations remain unsupported or unresolved.

The pilot must connect to the Claim vertical slice of Part VIII, including its
actual controls, production model operation, and authoritative reacquisition.
A successful isolated proof fixture cannot replace that application evidence.
Conversely, a working Claim browser test does not discharge missing proof
obligations. The compositional milestone requires both in their proper scopes.

The goal is not merely "green."

The goal is inspectable inheritance of guarantees through a real application.

** Developer experience and acceptance examples

The proof architecture succeeds only if ordinary application development remains
declaration-first and rejected programs are repairable.

Ordinary downstream development should ideally look like:

#+begin_example
declare domain/schema/rules/operations/choreo
        |
        v
Gesso elaborates and checks
        |
        +-- malformed -> immediate precise rejection
        |
        +-- coherent -> certified artifacts
                           |
                           v
                       projection
                           |
                           v
                     executable plan
#+end_example

The developer should mostly write the program and domain declarations.

Gesso should carry the proof bookkeeping.

Application developers supply domain truth where only the application can know
it; they should not need to become proof-assistant specialists to inherit Gesso's
generic guarantees.

The same ergonomic rule applies to nominal or opaque semantic categories.
Downstream web developers should not have to wrap routine IDs, strings, maps, or
domain values merely because Gesso internally distinguishes admitted semantic
stages.  Stronger wrappers or handles should appear at the comparatively small
number of boundaries where confusing same-shaped values would violate a real
invariant, and Gesso should construct them automatically wherever possible.

The first implementation tranche should not be considered useful until all of
these are possible.

*** Valid rule accepted

#+begin_src clojure
(rule claimable?
  (and
   (= request.status :open)
   (has-role? current-user :helper)))
#+end_src

assuming the relevant certified domain types.

*** Malformed rule rejected

#+begin_src clojure
(rule claimable?
  (< request.status 10))
#+end_src

*** Valid rule rejected at invalid distributed location

A browser cannot branch on a private authoritative fact it does not know.

*** Upstream schema change invalidates dependent certificate

Rename/remove =:claimed= and prove stale Request rule certificate cannot compose.

*** Model contract mismatch rejected

A choreography requiring =:claim-confirmed= cannot consume an operation contract
that no longer exports that outcome.

*** Forged certificate rejected

A same-shaped map with copied semantic-id and =:valid? true= cannot enter the
certified API.

*** Proof mutation rejected

Remove one premise from an otherwise valid derivation and require the proof kernel to
reject it.

*** Root proof report generated

One real HumanHelp operation produces a complete assumptions/guarantees/dependency
report.

*** The developer should not manage proof plumbing manually

Ordinary authoring should not require developers to:

- copy semantic IDs between namespaces;
- construct certificate maps;
- manually list transitive dependencies;
- handwrite derivation trees;
- understand proof-kernel-internal tags;
- write Lean.

The implementation should derive those artifacts from source-owned semantic
declarations wherever the dependency is mechanically knowable.

*** Explicit assumptions are still authored

When only the application knows a proposition, the developer may need to name
that proposition explicitly.

For example:

#+begin_example
application assertion:
    HumanHelp product policy treats this relationship as claim-eligible
#+end_example

That is legitimate.

The system should make the assertion visible in the root report rather than
forcing a fake derivation or an opaque escape hatch.

** Compositional certification invariant

At the end of the compositional-proof layer, the unified design requires:

#+begin_quote
Gesso constructs downstream semantic artifacts from certified interfaces rather
than namespace reputation, copied identifiers, map shape, or =:valid?= flags.
Raw declarations and certified values are distinct products.  Every certified
layer exports a minimal semantic interface consisting only of guarantees that
downstream reasoning may rely upon, together with explicit assumptions and
machine-checkable dependency identity.

Business-rule expressions use a closed typed semantic language that rejects
malformed expressions while still permitting coherent but product-wrong policy.
Intrinsic rule validity is not sufficient for distributed use: knowledge,
provenance, authority, refinement, and progression obligations must also hold at
the program point where the rule is consumed.

The certificate hierarchy follows semantic ownership from schema/domain and
relationships through rules, authoritative operation contracts, Choreo,
projection, =ExecutablePlan=, and whole-application realization.  Semantic
fingerprints establish normalized identity, not truth.  Stale or incompatible
dependency identities prevent downstream certification before browser
execution.  Compatibility beyond exact identity is admitted only through an
explicit checked compatibility relation.

A rich elaborator may generate normalized terms, obligations, and derivations,
but a deliberately smaller proof kernel or dedicated exact checker decides the
claims it is responsible for. Required obligation coverage and correspondence
from declared subject through projection to executable plan are explicit
checking or trust boundaries. An omitted obligation is not discharged merely
because every submitted derivation passes. Certificates are evidence accepted
by those checkers, not booleans, and optional proof/diagnostic artifacts never
influence runtime semantic transitions or authorization.

Assumptions remain scoped and transitively attributable to the guarantees that
use them. Discharge requires accepted evidence; converting failed checking into
an implicit assertion is forbidden. Semantic contract reuse does not preserve
evidence about a changed implementation or admitted assembly generation. Finite checking,
conditional theorems, runtime enforcement, and tests retain their actual scope;
incomplete checking never becomes a successful certificate.

Rejected programs retain stable structured diagnostics describing the failed
obligation, dependencies, available versus required type/knowledge/provenance/
authority facts, assumptions, and the downstream artifacts that were not
constructed.  A systematic mutation corpus demonstrates that malformed
schema/rule/knowledge/authority/choreography/certificate/application variants
stop at the intended boundary.

HumanHelp Request Claim is the first real compositional pilot.  Its assurance
graph extends from production model interfaces through Claim rules, operation
contract, choreography, projections, executable plan, HumanHelp
=ApplicationAssembly=, exact browser artifact, transport, trusted server/model
execution, settlement/progression, and authoritative reacquisition.  An upstream
semantic mismatch prevents construction of the stale downstream application
artifact, while application-specific policy assumptions remain visible rather
than being mislabeled as framework theorems.
#+end_quote

* Part X — Formal correspondence and system boundaries
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: part-10-formal-correspondence-and-system-boundaries
:END:

This part specifies the scope of stronger proof claims and their connection to
production, together with the trusted boundaries and multi-node contracts that
those claims must acknowledge. It consolidates the repeated formalization
material into one correspondence contract without treating test coverage as a
substitute for a theorem.

** Formal strengthening and system boundaries

This part connects the compositional architecture of Part IX to system-wide
assurance claims, external formalization, trusted implementation boundaries,
and physical multi-node realization. It introduces no second semantic layer.
Release/completion gates and the ordered realization roadmap follow in Parts XI
and XII. Historical plan ordering does not override the canonical contracts.

*** Assurance is layered rather than binary

Gesso does not have one global state named =proved=.

Different claims may legitimately have different evidence.

The accepted assurance classes are:

- *machine-proved / externally machine-checked* -- a theorem follows from an
  explicit formal semantics under named assumptions;
- *kernel-checked* -- a production proof term or derivation is accepted by the
  small trusted proof kernel;
- *exact-finite checked* -- the stated property was exhaustively checked over a
  finite semantic state space, with the scope of that claim stated precisely;
- *assembly-verified* -- a closed application graph/artifact correspondence
  check succeeded;
- *runtime-enforced* -- a concrete premise is validated or supplied at runtime;
- *trusted* -- correctness depends on an external implementation or boundary
  outside the current proof;
- *asserted* -- the application explicitly supplies a domain proposition Gesso
  cannot derive;
- *tested* -- behavioral evidence exists, but the property is not promoted to a
  stronger assurance class.

The root assurance report may contain all of these at once, with the meanings
fixed by [[#req-assurance-classification][Part I]]. Conditional liveness qualifies
a progress claim and its environmental premises; it is not an additional
evidence mechanism. A conditional-liveness claim can be theorem-backed, model-
checked, or supported by tests, and the report must say which.

The design must not collapse them into one marketing label.

*** Stronger proof never changes semantic ownership

Formalization may strengthen confidence in:

- type safety;
- knowledge/provenance use;
- projection preservation;
- certificate composition;
- browser-adapter transition invariants;
- progression algebra;
- stale-dependency rejection;
- whole-application correspondence.

It does not move HumanHelp business truth into Gesso.

For example, Gesso may prove that a distributed program invokes the declared
=:request/claim= operation only under the modeled knowledge/authority conditions.

It does not prove that HumanHelp product policy chose the correct Claim rule
unless that rule itself is separately specified and verified against a stronger
external requirement.

*** The final formal boundary follows the production semantics

The object of formal reasoning must remain the semantic object production uses.

The architecture rejects:

#+begin_example
formal model A
    |
proof of A
    |
handwritten production semantics B
#+end_example

when the intended correctness claim concerns B.

The desired direction is:

#+begin_example
explicit production executable semantics
        |
        +--> executable use
        |
        +--> model checking
        |
        +--> proof-term generation
        |
        +--> external formal correspondence
#+end_example

The same rule applies to projected plans, browser transition semantics, and
whole-application assembly.

The executable semantic core realizes production transitions. The proof kernel
checks derivations, while dedicated semantic checkers establish their particular
judgments through declared mechanisms. These roles may share canonical semantic
definitions without being one component or one assurance class. An external
proof-assistant kernel is another explicitly named checking boundary, not an
alternate application runtime. Correspondence connects the checked subject to
the production object; terminology alone does not establish that connection.

** System-wide assurance target matrix
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-system-assurance-targets
:END:

The complete architecture aims to establish the following guarantees.

Each guarantee must be accompanied by an honest assurance classification in the
implementation/root report.

The section states the desired property.

It does not imply that every property has already reached its strongest target
classification.

*** Choreography language and projection

The supported global choreography language has explicit transition semantics.

The system should establish that:

- a global choreography has a closed, inspectable semantic representation;
- projected machines arise from an accepted global choreography;
- the supported projection algorithm preserves the declared global/local trace
  or refinement relation;
- projected behavior does not invent undeclared communication;
- projection preserves every knowledge-producing mechanism required by local
  behavior;
- behavior-sensitive branch divergence is allowed only after the local role has
  justified knowledge of the required discriminator or an equivalent fact;
- unsupported semantic constructs are rejected or explicitly classified outside
  the proved subset rather than silently inheriting stronger guarantees.

Target evidence:

- explicit semantics;
- proof-kernel checking or a dedicated exact-model checker for the stated
  construction judgments;
- external theorem support for stable projection rules where practical.

*** Knowledge, provenance, and agreement

The system should establish that:

- senders know the values they send;
- consumers know the facts they consume;
- communication establishes knowledge only for the declared payload;
- authoritative observation establishes only the facts justified by the
  observed authoritative projection and its provenance/basis;
- deterministic derivation establishes only declared outputs from known inputs;
- asserted knowledge remains visibly asserted/trusted;
- weaker provenance cannot silently become stronger provenance;
- browser assertion alone cannot become authoritative provenance;
- agreement claims are justified through declared communication, compatible
  authoritative observation, checked derivation, or explicit assertion;
- agreement over evolving facts is scoped to a meaningful frontier rather than
  assumed from co-presence or shape equality;
- privacy/authorization may intentionally produce different valid knowledge
  projections for different actors.

Target evidence:

- proof-kernel-checkable knowledge/provenance rules for the closed semantic subset;
- runtime enforcement for concrete authoritative reads and authentication;
- application assertions where domain truth is external to Gesso.

*** Types, schemas, and rule use

The system should establish that:

- message payloads satisfy declared Malli/value schemas;
- machine contexts satisfy state schemas at checked boundaries;
- supported typed rule operators are used only on values in their semantic
  domains;
- unknown fields/variants are rejected;
- optional values are not dereferenced without a valid refinement;
- required closed matches are exhaustive;
- relationship predicates use valid endpoint/cardinality contracts;
- operation output fields cannot be consumed before they are produced;
- a rule that is intrinsically well typed is still rejected at a distributed
  program point when required knowledge, provenance, authority, refinement, or
  progression is absent.

Target evidence:

- runtime schemas;
- typed rule elaboration;
- kernel-checked semantic judgments;
- mutation tests for unsupported/malformed expressions.

*** Authoritative model transitions

The system should establish that:

- public model operations own authoritative business semantics;
- authoritative transitions advertised as atomic have one atomic commit boundary;
- failures established to precede commit cannot expose half of the promised
  atomic authoritative transition;
- failures after commit cannot reclassify or implicitly revert that transition;
- lost outcomes after transaction submission preserve participant-relative
  uncertainty rather than inventing rejection;
- known post-commit publication/settlement failure creates delivery/recovery
  obligations, not evidence that the mutation failed;
- FX inputs and outputs are explicit and do not rely on hidden context mutation;
- model operation contracts distinguish required capability, authority, typed
  input/outcome, atomicity, and trusted assumptions;
- Choreo depends on public model contracts rather than private model FX/graph
  internals.

Target evidence:

- runtime-enforced transaction guards;
- model/property tests;
- certified operation contracts;
- implementation conformance trusted initially where arbitrary Clojure bodies
  are not formally verified.

*** Progression and authoritative observation

The system should establish that:

- authoritative progression is distinct from domain revision, valid time,
  command identity, and browser execution/resource-instance identities;
- progression requirements are canonical;
- composition of progression requirements is conservative;
- commit-derived invalidation and reread preserve sufficient scoped progression
  requirements when delivered;
- loss between commit and publication follows the declared recovery contract,
  with the assumptions needed for eventual acquisition made explicit;
- advisory invalidation never fabricates authoritative content or claims that
  the recipient has already adopted the advertised progression;
- a raw browser-supplied basis is not proof of prior observation; any admitted
  observation receipt establishes only its explicitly validated issuance and
  scope contract, not delivery, installation, or human awareness;
- stale basis is exposed to model policy rather than interpreted by Gesso as an
  automatic conflict;
- convergence may skip intermediate advisory events when a newer authoritative
  observation satisfies the application's semantics.

Target evidence:

- explicit progression algebra;
- XTDB integration tests;
- exact finite/proof checks for algebraic properties where practical;
- trusted XTDB consistency contract.

*** Optimism

The system should establish that:

- optimistic state is distinguishable from authoritative state;
- every optimistic projection carries semantic command and authoritative-basis
  provenance;
- provisional state cannot increase server authority;
- every provisional trajectory has a declared resolution/recovery path;
- direct settlement loss permits later authoritative supersession through the
  declared recovery path under its availability and scheduling assumptions;
- current-state supersession does not by itself settle command attribution;
- newer authoritative installation revokes stale rollback rights;
- multiple browsers may hold conflicting provisional projections without
  creating multiple authoritative histories;
- a semantic optimistic operation never silently downgrades to an anonymous
  ordinary request because basis/command/plan data is absent.

Target evidence:

- portable transition semantics;
- browser adapter invariant checking;
- whole-application rendered-surface enforcement;
- real Chromium adversarial scheduling tests.

*** Browser transition safety

The production browser semantic adapter should itself be the checked transition
system.

The system should establish that:

- every semantically active async effect has one declared owner or explicit
  shared-ownership class;
- execution-private callbacks are admitted only for the current
  execution identity and relevant resource-instance ownership state;
- stale or duplicate events cannot resume an illegal machine state;
- stale callbacks cannot acquire validity merely because a logical target still
  exists;
- authoritative install cannot be undone by an obsolete provisional callback;
- semantic retirement revokes callback authority before best-effort physical
  cleanup;
- physical cancellation is an optimization rather than the sole safety gate;
- fragment coordination satisfies its stated bound on logical active/queued
  slots; retained metadata and retired physical requests have separately stated
  bounds or abstractions;
- shared fragment/subscription events use their declared owner-relative
  admission path without fabricated command execution identity;
- deferred physical mutation revalidates the relevant effect permission before
  changing managed DOM, not only when its callback begins;
- continuity may preserve local UI state but cannot alter domain/choreography
  semantics;
- incompatible stale projected plans retire and use the declared recovery or
  authoritative reconstruction path, preserving the product's acquisition and
  adoption policy rather than treating reconnect as automatic permission.

Target evidence:

- exact transition-system/model checking;
- property tests over adversarial event orders;
- JVM/Node/Chromium conformance;
- real browser integration for FFI/HTMX assumptions.

*** Portable execution and artifact identity

The system should establish that:

- the same portable semantic transition core runs under JVM, Node, and Chromium
  for the supported subset;
- production and proof-enabled tests execute the same canonical
  =ExecutablePlan=;
- proof/diagnostic sidecars are runtime-nonsemantic;
- exact sidecar lookup requires exact executable-artifact identity;
- compatibility identity remains distinct from exact artifact identity;
- generated browser artifacts correspond exactly to the current required browser
  manifest before the application is served.

Target evidence:

- deterministic plan/artifact identities;
- conformance tests;
- build/startup closure checks;
- structural noninterference by runtime API shape, with optional diagnostics
  isolated from semantic control flow and failures;
- equivalence for the same initial state and admitted event stream, without
  claiming identical wall-clock scheduling or external resource behavior.

*** Whole-application closure

The system should establish that every exposed Choreo-managed affordance has a
coherent path through:

#+begin_example
view affordance
    -> semantic operation
    -> verified trajectory
    -> projected browser plan
    -> exact generated artifact
    -> declared transport
    -> trusted route
    -> trusted server operation
    -> authoritative model operation
    -> settlement/progression
    -> authoritative acquisition
    -> renderable authoritative projection
#+end_example

The system should reject:

- unknown semantic operations;
- missing browser plans;
- stale generated artifacts;
- missing/incompatible transport;
- missing trusted route/server operation;
- missing required server capability;
- missing acquisition path;
- anonymous ordinary HTMX use of an assembled semantic route;
- implicit semantic downgrade;
- duplicate/incompatible identities.

Target evidence:

- =ApplicationAssembly= closure checking;
- runtime rendered-surface enforcement;
- lifecycle-enforced preflight before serving;
- real-browser acceptance through the same admitted assembly generation.

Closure quantifies over declared affordances. Independent required-capability
acceptance must also fail when a promised action is omitted. Production
entrypoint enforcement must demonstrate that the checked assembly and artifact
are those actually used. These three claims remain separate under
[[#req-application-assurance-claims]].

*** Browser/server authority boundary

Under named authentication/model/XTDB assumptions, arbitrary browser behavior
must not grant additional authoritative privilege.

The browser may propose:

- operation;
- arguments;
- command ID;
- observed basis;
- correlation metadata.

The browser may not establish:

- authenticated principal;
- server role entitlement;
- authoritative provenance;
- current model authorization;
- protected read/subscription permission;
- trusted server machine state.

Target evidence:

- trusted-side operation registry;
- authenticated principal binding;
- runtime authorization;
- adversarial browser tests;
- semantic authority-flow theorem for the modeled protocol boundary.

*** Liveness and convergence

The architecture should support explicit conditional claims such as:

#+begin_quote
If relevant authority eventually becomes reachable, the actor continues to
execute, the declared acquisition/adoption policy permits the required work,
any required user action occurs, the scheduler fairly selects enabled recovery,
required messages/reads eventually succeed, and the authoritative world is
quiescent long enough, then the specifically named presentation or agreement
obligation resolves. Command attribution additionally requires command-specific
outcome evidence when current-state observation is insufficient.
#+end_quote

The system must not infer unconditional liveness merely because:

- a terminal path exists;
- a timeout transition exists;
- a refresh route exists;
- a message could theoretically arrive.

Every liveness/convergence claim names its environment assumptions and the exact
obligation that eventually resolves. A reachable terminal state proves the
possibility of progress, not eventual progress on every fair execution. Any
argument over all fair executions must justify the fairness and progress steps
it uses. Safety must continue to hold when liveness premises fail; remaining
unresolved is preferable to inventing an outcome or permission.

Target evidence:

- graph/state-machine analysis;
- conditional theorem statements;
- integration/fault testing for the external assumptions.

** Framework theorem families

The following are the principal theorem families over the stable semantic
definitions. Their subjects and evidence mechanisms must be stated separately;
the executable semantic core is not itself the proof kernel.

*** Certified composition soundness

If certificate B is accepted using certificate A, every premise B attributes to
A must follow from A's exported guarantees under checked scope/substitution and
compatibility rules. Additional premises admitted at B remain explicit
assumptions of the resulting conclusion, not guarantees attributed to A.
Required obligation coverage and subject binding follow Part IX. A conditional
composition theorem does not establish its external premises or the conformity
of arbitrary implementation bodies.

*** Business-rule type safety

A certified rule cannot apply a supported operator to values outside the
operator's declared semantic domain.

*** Rule-use knowledge safety

If a certified projected role branches/acts on a rule result, all facts required
to evaluate that rule are justified by the role's certified knowledge at that
point, modulo named assumptions.

*** Provenance safety

A stronger provenance class is never derived solely from a weaker class unless a
declared inference rule permits it.

In particular, browser assertion alone cannot become authoritative provenance.

*** Authority derivation and non-escalation safety

No projected/browser-supplied semantic artifact can increase the authority of the
authenticated principal without a trusted server/model rule that establishes the
capability.

*** Projection preservation

For the supported Choreo subset, accepted local projections preserve the stated
global trace/refinement property.

*** Executable-plan provenance

Every certified production plan corresponds to one accepted projection under
the recorded semantic dependencies and plan-format version.

*** Stale certificate safety

A certificate whose required semantic dependency identity no longer matches
cannot be used to produce a certified downstream artifact.

*** Progression preservation

The existing progression algebra should eventually participate in the same proof
story:

- requirements are canonical;
- composition is conservative;
- success/failure rules preserve unsatisfied requirements appropriately;
- advisory invalidation does not fabricate authoritative progression.

*** Browser semantic adapter safety

Existing adapter state-machine properties should eventually be connectable to
the root proof report while the DOM/HTMX/browser shell remains an explicit
trusted/tested realization boundary.

** Formal proof strategy and production correspondence
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-formal-production-correspondence
:END:

External formalization follows the stable production semantics. The intended
progression is explicit semantic data, compositional Clojure certificates, a
small proof kernel, proof-producing elaboration, stable proof-kernel semantics,
external soundness theorems, and checked production/formal correspondence.

Formalization aims to reduce trust in Gesso's own verifier. It does not replace
Clojure, Malli, XTDB, HTMX, or Chromium, and does not move HumanHelp business
policy into the framework. This is a target architecture, not a statement that
its theorems or correspondence mechanisms already exist.

*** External proof assistant role

Lean is the leading candidate, but the architectural role matters more than the
particular proof assistant. Initial formal objects should remain smaller than
the full implementation:

- proposition syntax and semantic type judgments;
- knowledge/provenance entailment;
- authority/capability entailment;
- the declared progression requirement algebra;
- certificate dependency composition;
- selected transition invariants and projection properties.

Application developers continue to author Clojure/Clojure data and Gesso's
closed semantic languages. Ordinary development does not require Lean, tactic
knowledge, manual proof trees, or hand translation of HumanHelp rules into a
second language. The framework derives the relevant formal objects where their
meaning is mechanically available.

*** Exact claim and correspondence direction

A theorem about a formal checker is insufficient if production accepts different
derivations or executes a different plan. Every transferred claim must identify:

- the formal subject and proposition, including assumptions and supported subset;
- the concrete production object and its immutable identity/version;
- the representation relation between formal and production subjects;
- the checking, translation, or refinement relation used for transfer;
- the direction and scope of that relation;
- the remaining implementation and toolchain trust premises.

For sound admission, a typical required direction is: whenever the production
boundary accepts a subject for guarantee G, the corresponding formal judgment
holds under the recorded premises. Agreement on rejected examples alone does
not establish this direction. Completeness, meaning acceptance of every valid
supported subject, is a different property. A sound checker may be conservative;
usability and supported-feature acceptance must still prevent “reject all” from
being treated as a successful implementation.

For execution safety, a trace/refinement argument must account for every
relevant concrete behavior and its observable meaning. Mere acceptance-set
agreement does not prove that the runtime executes the certified plan correctly.
For liveness, correspondence must additionally address divergence, scheduling,
and fairness as needed; a safety simulation that allows arbitrary stuttering
cannot alone establish progress.

*** Correspondence mechanisms and their evidence

Select the smallest mechanism that demonstrably reduces the trusted gap. The
following mechanisms have different assurance implications.

| Mechanism | What it can establish | Remaining obligation |
|-----------+-----------------------+----------------------|
| Externally checked proof terms | The particular admitted proposition follows from accepted premises | Bind proof, subject, dependencies, and actual production artifact; enforce the check |
| Generated or extracted checker | Implementation originates from the formal definition | Justify translation/extraction and execution toolchain, or identify them as trusted |
| Mechanically proved comparison/refinement | A stated relation holds for all objects in the proved domain | Identify domain, direction, representation relation, and assumptions |
| Exhaustive comparison over a finite domain | Acceptance/results agree throughout that specified finite domain | Do not extend to unbounded inputs without a sound coverage argument |
| Generated conformance vectors | Tested implementations agree on those vectors | Tests alone do not prove correspondence outside the exercised cases |

Using every currently generated vector, or covering every inference-rule tag,
does not establish coverage of all rule parameters, combinations, recursive
proof trees, malformed encodings, or production paths. Such results remain
conformance tests unless a separate argument establishes exhaustive coverage
for the claimed domain. More than one mechanism may be needed.

A source review saying “the Clojure rule looks like the Lean theorem” is review
evidence, not formal correspondence. Similarly, using a formally specified
algorithm does not prove a handwritten implementation conforms to it.

*** Bind formal checking to the admitted production artifact

The correspondence path must bind the normalized subject, complete required
obligations, derivation, dependencies, proof-kernel semantics, and result to the object
actually admitted. The boundaries identified in [[#req-proof-kernel-boundary]]
remain relevant: normalization, projection, executable lowering, and runtime
realization each need appropriate checked correspondence or named trust.

An independently valid proof about subject A must not authorize construction of
subject B. Copying A's identity onto B is insufficient. Build/startup must bind
the admitted plan and generated artifact to the admitted assembly generation actually served,
following Part VII. Immutable references or revalidation must prevent a later
mutable lookup from substituting a different object after checking.

If an external check is a required construction gate, unavailable checking,
timeout, unsupported features, or invalid evidence prevent that admission. The
system may retain an already admitted valid assembly generation under the declared
lifecycle contract; it must not silently waive the requested certification
profile. Proof sidecars remain unnecessary for runtime semantic decisions after
admission and never become authorization capabilities.

*** Formal work follows stable semantics

Make the semantics explicit, use them in production, exercise adversarial cases,
stabilize the relevant checking rules, and then formalize them. The aim is to
reduce a specific trusted surface rather than repeatedly translating unstable
representations or encoding the whole implementation wholesale.

The selected formal scope may grow incrementally. Each report names the actual
subset proved and the properties still checked, tested, or trusted. A theorem
for one primitive cannot silently cover every expression using other primitives.

*** Finite checking and abstraction

Exact finite exploration establishes a property over its specified finite model.
The model must name its initial states, transitions, data domains, property, and
exploration completeness. A timeout or partially explored graph is incomplete
evidence, not a successful exact check or a counterexample.

Checking resource-instance counter values 0 through 3 establishes only that
bounded result unless a sound abstraction argument covers all relevant concrete
resource instances, counter scopes, and reuse/retirement behavior. Opaque instance
identities may require a different abstraction; no universal counter is assumed.
The same
applies to command identities, progression tokens, dependency depth, queue
metadata, and retired physical effects. Bounded queue slots do not imply a
finite overall state space.

An abstraction supporting a concrete safety claim must preserve the relevant
initial states and simulate all concrete transitions needed for that claim,
with the abstract invariant implying the concrete safety property. Merely
mapping each state to some abstract state is insufficient. A liveness transfer
needs the additional progress/fairness/divergence conditions its theorem uses.
An abstract counterexample may require concretization before being reported as
a realizable production failure.

*** Evidence package and maintenance

A formal claim is reviewable only when its evidence identifies:

- theorem statement and formal semantic definitions;
- proof/checker versions, relevant options, and any admitted axioms;
- normalized subject, dependency closure, and artifact identities;
- representation/correspondence definitions and their evidence class;
- exact finite bounds or abstraction soundness evidence where used;
- the production boundary enforcing admission;
- open assumptions and excluded behavior;
- reproducible checking instructions and the observed result.

A changed semantic rule, primitive, checker, translation, or production binding
must trigger the rechecking appropriate to its dependency edges. Unchanged
interface-level proofs may remain reusable while implementation correspondence
needs renewed evidence. The maintenance contract must not confuse a theorem's
continuing mathematical validity with applicability to a changed release.

** Trusted computing base, security boundaries, and failure modes
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-trusted-system-boundaries
:END:

The formal architecture is credible only when the trusted computing base is
named and kept small.

*** Assurance ledger

For each release/milestone, the root assurance report should identify:

- proof-kernel namespaces/size;
- elaborator/compiler namespaces;
- runtime-enforced boundary namespaces;
- trusted external libraries/systems;
- application assertions;
- properties machine-proved;
- properties kernel-checked;
- properties exact-finite checked;
- properties assembly-verified;
- properties runtime-enforced;
- properties supported only by tests;
- exact implementation, dependency, and semantic/checker versions in scope;
- correspondence and admission boundaries that bind the evidence to production;
- conditional-liveness premises and unresolved obligations.

Namespace size is useful audit information, not a proof of soundness. The ledger
must distinguish the logical trusted base from the larger runtime, security,
and availability dependencies. Each claim identifies the relevant subset;
listing every dependency without saying which claim relies on it is insufficient.

A smaller explicitly named TCB is preferable to a much larger system described
vaguely as formal.

*** Typical trusted/external boundaries

The exact list evolves with implementation, but likely boundaries include:

- JVM/Clojure runtime;
- ClojureScript/JavaScript engine;
- browser primitive behavior;
- supported HTMX lifecycle contract;
- Aleph/Ring transport implementation;
- authentication/session implementation;
- XTDB transaction/consistency implementation;
- operating system/network behavior;
- external proof assistant and its proof-checking kernel implementation where used;
- parsing, serialization, normalization, extraction/code generation, and build
  tooling wherever their correctness is not independently checked;
- primitive operator semantics and cryptographic assumptions used for identity
  or authenticated evidence.

These are not ignored.

They are named premises.

Real integration and adversarial testing exists partly to challenge them.

*** Certificate system does not replace runtime authorization

A malicious browser may forge:

- serialized maps;
- DOM;
- JavaScript;
- headers;
- semantic IDs;
- plan-looking objects;
- certificate-looking objects;
- role/principal fields;
- basis/provenance claims.

Therefore:

- server authority comes from trusted server context and current model policy;
- endpoints resolve operations through trusted registries;
- protected reads/subscriptions remain server-authorized;
- cached/persisted certificates re-enter through structural/semantic validation;
- artifact signing may protect integrity but does not prove semantic correctness;
- a structural certificate about a route never substitutes for validating its
  concrete invocation before protected execution.

The security claim is relative to its specified threat model and boundary. A
browser may propose arbitrary bytes; hostile code already holding server-side
transaction authority is a different trust condition. Server authorization must
hold even if checked rendering is bypassed. Authentication, request-origin/CSRF
controls where relevant, input/resource limits, and protected-data disclosure
follow the actual transport contract; a non-escalation theorem about the modeled
protocol does not automatically prove the security of the entire web stack.

*** Hashes are identities, not proofs

A checked digest provides correspondence evidence for specified bytes or
normalized content under the canonicalization and collision-resistance
assumptions. A digest string copied into unverified metadata provides no such
binding by itself.

It does not establish the truth of the proposition associated with those bytes.

The architecture always distinguishes:

#+begin_example
identity evidence
    from
semantic proof evidence
#+end_example

*** Elaborator/checker shared-bug risk

A rich elaborator and a checker implemented from the same misunderstanding may
share a bug.

Mitigations include:

- deliberately small proof kernel;
- adversarial malformed derivations;
- independent rule tests;
- external formalization;
- production/formal correspondence.

The purpose of the independent proof-kernel/formal step is specifically to reduce the
amount of semantic correctness that depends on trusting the compiler that
generated the evidence.

*** Certificate forgery

Same-shaped map construction must not enter certified APIs.

A public API that accepts:

#+begin_src clojure
{:semantic-id X
 :valid? true}
#+end_src

as equivalent to a checker-issued certified value defeats the architecture.

Certified values use checker-controlled constructors and validated persistence
boundaries.

*** Circular trust

An operation contract cannot prove its own implementation merely by declaring
the postcondition it wants.

Likewise:

- a rule cannot certify itself;
- a certificate cannot accept itself as its own dependency;
- a proof of projection cannot assume the preservation theorem it is intended to
  establish.

Certificate dependencies remain acyclic. Supported recursive semantics use an
explicit checked fixed-point/induction rule within a component, with a soundness
argument for that rule. They do not permit circular certificate justification.
Unsupported cycles reject.

*** Assertion inflation

=:asserted= is a legitimate proof-DAG leaf.

It is not an escape hatch for every difficult obligation.

Root reports should count and surface assertions prominently so an application
whose "proof" is mostly assertion is visibly different from one with derived
guarantees. Counts are diagnostic information, not a confidence score: one
central opaque premise may matter more than many minor assertions. Reports must
show which guarantees depend on each premise. Required proof-kernel obligations cannot
be converted into assertions merely because checking them failed.

*** Semantic-fingerprint under-approximation

If normalization omits a semantically relevant field, a stale certificate may
appear current.

Fingerprint normalization therefore needs adversarial mutation tests.

*** Semantic-fingerprint over-approximation

If normalization includes irrelevant implementation/source details, harmless
edits invalidate too much of the proof DAG and developers are incentivized to
bypass certification.

Semantic interfaces should therefore be minimal.

*** Proof artifacts must remain noninterfering

Proof sidecars, derivation trees, theorem metadata, and diagnostics never become
inputs to production transition decisions.

A proof system that changes runtime meaning when enabled has created a second
semantic channel.

*** "Verified" remains qualified

Public reports should prefer specific classifications:

- machine-proved;
- kernel-checked;
- exact-finite checked;
- assembly-verified;
- runtime-enforced;
- trusted;
- asserted;
- tested.

The unqualified word =verified= should not be allowed to hide materially
different evidence. Conditional-liveness qualifications remain attached to the
relevant claim alongside, rather than in place of, its evidence classification.

*** Arbitrary Clojure is not the formal target

The strongest proof effort concerns:

- the closed semantic rule language;
- semantic interfaces;
- choreography;
- projection;
- adapter transition semantics;
- certificate composition.

It does not attempt to prove arbitrary host-language behavior by pretending all
Clojure is inside the small logic.

Implementation conformance outside the closed semantic subset stays explicitly
trusted/tested until separately constrained or verified.

** Validation ladder

Stronger formal assurance does not remove the need for concrete testing layers.

Retain:

- unit tests;
- property tests;
- exact finite-state exhaustive checks;
- JVM/Node/Chromium portable conformance;
- real HTMX/SSE/EventSource integration;
- adversarial browser tests;
- XTDB integration tests;
- multi-node tests when multi-node Live exists;
- generated-artifact freshness tests.

Their role becomes clearer:

#+begin_example
formal proof
  -> semantic theorem

certificate checking
  -> downstream theorem composition

runtime checks
  -> concrete premises

integration/adversarial tests
  -> scoped evidence about shell correspondence and external assumptions
#+end_example

*** Evidence layers are complementary

A useful failure can live outside the theorem boundary.

For example:

- a proof may establish adapter execution/resource-instance isolation;
- a Chromium test may reveal that an HTMX lifecycle event is normalized
  incorrectly;
- a property test may find an elaborator bug before the proof kernel sees its output;
- an XTDB integration test may reveal an incorrect assumption about snapshot
  propagation;
- a HumanHelp multi-client test may reveal a missing whole-application edge.

The correction may occur in:

- semantics;
- implementation;
- trusted-assumption definition;
- application assembly;
- application policy.

The framework should not distort the proof boundary merely to make every bug a
theorem violation. Acceptance records the first enforcing boundary, the actual
admitted assembly generation, and the evidence scope. Expected network/console failures
in fault scenarios are asserted precisely; unrelated failures still fail the
scenario. A test-only wrapper rejecting before the real boundary is reached is
not evidence that production enforces that boundary.

** Multi-node realization and authoritative rendezvous
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-multi-node-realization
:END:

Multi-node deployment realizes the semantic distinctions already established.

It does not introduce a second authority model.
