*** Roles, actors, hosts, and authorities remain distinct

When several Aleph nodes exist:

- a role remains a semantic choreography role;
- an actor remains a role/principal realization;
- a host remains a physical process/node;
- an authority remains the trusted semantic owner of authoritative facts or
  operations.

A concrete node may temporarily realize an authority projection.

The node itself does not become semantic authority.

For example:

#+begin_example
role:
    :request-authority

host for invocation:
    web-node A

retry host:
    web-node C

semantic authority:
    [:authority :request R]
#+end_example

Host replacement must not change command identity or authoritative meaning.
The new host must recover or receive the actual command identity through its
trusted protocol; physical relocation does not reconstruct missing intent.
Retry safety follows the model's explicit idempotency/deduplication and retention
contract. A node-local cache or the same command string alone cannot establish
cross-node exactly-once effects. Outcome loss after submission can remain
unknown even when another node can read current authoritative state.

*** Durable model state remains the ordinary rendezvous point

For ordinary HumanHelp cross-user interactions:

#+begin_example
Alice browser
    |
Aleph node A
    |
Request authoritative operation
    |
XTDB commit
    |
distributed advisory invalidation
    |
Aleph node C
    |
Bob browser receives advisory
    |
product policy or explicit Refresh authorizes acquisition/adoption
    |
authorized authoritative reread
#+end_example

Alice and Bob do not require a persistent cross-node participant-message process
merely because they interact with the same Request.

The durable Request fact is the rendezvous point.

*** Distributed invalidation is not participant messaging

A distributed invalidation says approximately:

#+begin_quote
Relevant authority may have changed. A future acquisition must satisfy the
advertised scope/progression requirement; the application's policy determines
when acquisition and adoption are permitted.
#+end_quote

A choreography participant message says approximately:

#+begin_quote
This execution received this declared message.
#+end_quote

A future implementation may transport both through related infrastructure.

Their semantics remain different.

*** Direct participant routing is opt-in

A genuinely ephemeral protocol may require receipt of a participant message
itself to carry semantic meaning.

That use case may introduce:

- distributed actor-interest registration;
- message routing;
- correlation;
- duplicate/loss semantics;
- retry/delivery policy;
- authenticated routing, protected payload disclosure, and recipient admission;
- resource-instance retirement and the applicable compatibility contract across
  host replacement or a change of admitted assembly generation.

Delivery acknowledgments, broker persistence, execution admission, and domain
commit are distinct facts. Any stronger delivery guarantee must name its scope
and recovery assumptions rather than inheriting them from the broker's label.

Those mechanisms should be added when a real choreography requires them.

They must not redefine ordinary authoritative convergence.

*** First physical multi-node topology may be simple

The first useful multi-node environment does not need sophisticated
orchestration.

A development harness may use, as concretely useful:

- multiple Aleph nodes;
- HAProxy or another load balancer;
- shared XTDB/object-store dependencies;
- Valkey or another Live invalidation-routing substrate;
- Redpanda or another log only where a concrete requirement warrants it.

The service list is implementation-specific.

The semantic gate is real host replacement and cross-node propagation through
the same trusted model/protocol boundaries. Naming services is not evidence of
those properties. The selected topology must record the actual dependency
versions and supported consistency/durability contracts; this document neither
requires every listed service nor certifies an unspecified deployment.

*** Multi-node assumptions enter the assurance graph

Once the physical implementation exists, the root assurance report should name
the contracts needed for:

- host replacement;
- distributed invalidation routing;
- interest registration;
- post-commit publication;
- duplicate publication;
- lost publication;
- stale routing knowledge;
- reconnect through another node;
- node-local process loss;
- progression-safe reread from another node;
- command identity/deduplication scope and retention where retry guarantees rely
  on them;
- cross-node session/principal binding and compatible application realizations
  under the declared plan/protocol and admitted assembly contracts;
- acquisition/adoption policy across reconnect and initial reconstruction.

Authority committed while publication was lost must remain discoverable through
the implemented [[#req-post-commit-recovery][post-commit recovery contract]]. If
that contract relies on explicit Refresh, the liveness claim includes that user
action. Reconnect alone is not evidence that lost advisory information has been
recovered. A lagging node must not label an insufficient read as satisfying the
requested progression merely because it returned successfully.

These assumptions arise from the actual implementation.

They should not be invented speculatively before the topology exists.

*** Required multi-node acceptance evidence

The multi-node milestone must identify the actual topology and test at least:

- a mutation on one node becoming authoritatively observable through another;
- an already connected remote browser receiving advisory without unauthorized
  new-Request adoption, followed by successful permitted Refresh;
- a node lost before submission versus one lost after submission/commit, with
  participant uncertainty and any permitted retry handled distinctly;
- crash after commit before publication, followed by the declared recovery path;
- duplicate or reordered advisory delivery preserving scoped outstanding debt;
- reconnect through a different node preserving the declared adoption policy;
- a read unable to meet the required progression preserving recovery rather than
  falsely claiming current authority;
- current authentication and authorization at the replacement host;
- incompatible old/new plan versions following the selected compatibility or
  retirement contract.

These cases use real node/transport boundaries where the claimed failure depends
on physical deployment. Portable model tests remain complementary evidence.
The report separates safety under interruption from progress after the named
recovery conditions hold. A single successful load-balanced request does not
establish the multi-node milestone.

*** Multi-node does not require durable suspended choreography machines

A node may disappear while the durable business process survives.

Ordinary long waits remain represented as authoritative model state and later
short choreographies.

Persisting arbitrary suspended choreography-machine state across process death is
a separate opt-in facility with its own:

- persistence;
- versioning;
- migration;
- ownership;
- recovery;
- delivery semantics.

*** Proof work and multi-node work can overlap

Full multi-node productionization is not a prerequisite for compositional proof
hardening.

Conversely, a small real multi-node prototype may expose missing semantic
distinctions that should be corrected before deeper formalization.

The correct relationship is iterative:

#+begin_example
stable semantic model
    |
small real multi-node realization
    |
new failure evidence
    |
refine named assumptions / semantics if necessary
    |
proof/root report incorporates the actual contracts
#+end_example

The semantic invariant remains constant:

#+begin_quote
Physical topology may change; semantic authority does not derive from host
identity.
#+end_quote


** Formal correspondence and system-boundary invariant

#+begin_quote
A formal theorem applies to production only through its stated representation,
checking, or refinement relation. Conformance vectors remain tests unless their
coverage justifies a stronger claim. Complete obligation coverage, immutable
subject/artifact binding, and explicit translation assumptions prevent a proof
of one object from being attributed to another. Exact finite checking does not
silently become an unbounded theorem, and safety correspondence does not
silently establish liveness.

The root report preserves the evidence class, open assumptions, trusted boundary,
and applicable subject/artifact identities and admitted assembly generation of
each claim where relevant. Runtime authorization and protected
acquisition remain necessary regardless of certificates. Multi-node placement
changes hosts, not authority; retries, publication recovery, current reads, and
command attribution follow their explicit contracts. HumanHelp's selective
adoption policy survives these realizations. Operational success, compositional
certification, external formal correspondence, and multi-node completion remain
distinct milestones with distinct evidence.
#+end_quote

* Part XI — Release and completion
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: part-11-release-and-completion
:END:

This part defines evidence reuse, candidate admission, release reports, global
non-goals, and four distinct completion scopes. It makes a release claim
reviewable against the actual artifacts and required capabilities without
requiring every later proof milestone before an operational application exists.

** Incremental certification, build, and release
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-release-evidence
:END:

A proof system that rebuilds the world after every edit will be resisted.

Design for incremental recomputation:

- normalized semantic identities, certified dependency identities, checker/rule
  profiles, and relevant assumptions determine certificate cache eligibility;
- direct dependency identities identify what must be reconsidered;
- unchanged semantic interfaces retain dependent validity where safe;
- diagnostic/source metadata can change independently of semantic identity;
- cache entries are re-admitted under the current checker/version and
  certification-profile contract;
- cache corruption cannot create a certificate the kernel would otherwise
  reject.

Correctness dominates cache hit rate.

The build system is where semantic identity, proof dependency identity,
whole-application closure, and exact browser artifact correspondence become one
release discipline.

*** Deterministic semantic regeneration

For unchanged normalized semantic inputs, regeneration should produce stable:

- semantic identities;
- certificate dependency identities;
- projected plan identity where representation is deterministic;
- normalized application assembly identity;
- browser assembly manifest identity.

Determinism is relative to the complete declared inputs, including relevant
normalization, compiler, dependency, and format versions. It does not claim that
semantic equality alone makes all compiler output byte-identical. Exact artifact
bytes have their own checked identity and build binding. Build timestamps or
other diagnostic metadata may vary only where their exclusion from the relevant
identity is explicit.

Diagnostic-only source metadata may change without forcing semantic
re-certification when the normalized interface is unchanged.

*** Release gate

A production release must pass the gates for its declared supported scope and
claimed milestone. It must not be admitted with any of the following defects
within that required scope:

- unresolved obligations required by its selected certification profile;
- stale semantic dependency identities;
- invalid proof/kernel derivations;
- stale browser artifact relative to the current assembly manifest;
- application assembly closure errors;
- a missing mandatory preflight lifecycle module;
- generated runtime that is stale relative to authored ClojureScript;
- proof/diagnostic data included as a runtime semantic decision input;
- missing required operational capabilities or failed independent acceptance;
- a production entrypoint that bypasses the required construction/render gates.

The operational milestone does not require completion of the later compositional
or external-formal milestones. Conversely, a build requesting a stronger profile
cannot silently fall back to operational assurance when certification fails.
Admitted trusted premises remain visible; they are not unresolved proof tasks
unless the selected profile requires their discharge.

Release scope and required capabilities are declared before gate evaluation.
Removing a failing operation or shrinking the proof subset during that evaluation
cannot preserve the original completion claim. A deliberately narrower release
must be identified as such, with its missing capabilities and excluded claims.
This does not redefine the canonical HumanHelp milestone into a smaller target.

Ordinary runtime test gates remain required. A failed, skipped, unavailable,
timed-out, or incomplete required check is not a pass. Reports distinguish a
counterexample or invalid artifact from infrastructure failure and unsupported
checking. An expected rejection counts as success only for a negative test
whose expected boundary and diagnostic were specified in advance.

The release gate produces a concrete candidate and evidence; it is not itself
a deployment action. Activation follows the generation/lifecycle contracts in
Part VII and must use the artifact that the gate admitted.

*** Incremental invalidation is dependency-driven

When semantic identity A changes:

#+begin_example
A changed
    |
direct dependents of A reconsidered
    |
only affected downstream proof DAG rebuilt
#+end_example

The system should not rebuild unrelated artifacts merely because they live in the
same source file or Git commit.

At the same time, cache correctness outranks speed.

A cache hit never bypasses kernel/checker validation required by the current
checker version. Reuse may avoid repeated work through an admitted immutable
in-process result or another explicitly checked cache contract. Merely finding
a stored successful report is insufficient.

Implementation changes require separate consideration. An unchanged semantic
interface can preserve downstream contract-level derivations while invalidating
conformance or test evidence for a changed handler, provider, compiler, or
runtime artifact. Reuse follows each claim's actual dependencies under
[[#req-certificate-composition]], rather than a blanket semantic-ID shortcut.

*** Cached certificate corruption fails closed

A corrupted cache entry must not become accepted simply because its filename or
cache key matches.

The cache stores candidate evidence.

The current trusted checker still performs the admission checks required for
that certificate class: normalized subject, structure, dependencies, assumptions,
checker/rule compatibility, and derivation validity where required. A copied
semantic ID or stale success flag cannot override those checks.

Corruption may reject the candidate or trigger fresh reconstruction from valid
inputs; it must never relax acceptance. Cached values crossing serialization
boundaries regain admission only through the validation specified in Part IX.

*** Root release report

A production build should be able to emit one machine-readable and human-readable
root report describing at least:

- application/assembly semantic identity;
- certified artifact classes;
- proof-kernel/checker version;
- unresolved obligations, expected zero for certified production scope;
- explicit assumptions/assertions;
- whole-application closure status;
- current browser-artifact identity and correspondence;
- runtime/test/conformance gate results;
- TCB classification;
- declared milestone, supported subset, and certification profile;
- concrete source/build provenance, admitted generation, and dependency versions;
- identities of the actual tested and served artifacts;
- required user-capability acceptance and production-enforcement evidence;
- exclusions, unresolved product choices, and environmental/liveness premises;
- result and evidence reference for each required gate, including incomplete
  or skipped outcomes.

Each evidence reference identifies the subject and configuration it covers.
Passing tests for a different fixture assembly, old artifact, or shadow model
cannot establish current release behavior. Reuse is permitted only where its
applicability is justified by unchanged dependencies or a checked compatibility
relation. A prior timestamp or a matching operation name is not that relation.

The report is release evidence describing what was built and which assurance
claims support it.  It is not runtime authorization and does not become a
semantic input merely because the build that produced it was admitted.

*** Candidate integrity and activation

The admitted candidate binds declarations, prepared server realizations,
providers, routes, normalized semantic/certificate identities, executable plans,
manifest, and actual generated bytes according to the acyclic construction
phases in [[#req-assembly-construction-phases]]. Build and test evidence must not
be paired with a different artifact at deployment time.

Unchanged normalized inputs produce deterministic identities where claimed,
including certificate content/identity when its encoding is deterministic.
Diagnostic source metadata remains separate. Where exact reproducible build
bytes are claimed, the toolchain and all byte-affecting inputs must also be
controlled; otherwise the report claims semantic reproducibility and records
the actual artifact bytes separately.

A dependency or mutable binding changed after admission requires the appropriate
revalidation before activation. A failed candidate may leave a still-valid old
generation running under the lifecycle contract; it may not partially replace
that generation. Old browser plans use the explicitly supported compatibility
or retirement/recovery path.

Reactivating an older executable generation does not roll back committed domain
state. Any release involving model/schema evolution must identify its supported
state compatibility and recovery constraints before claiming that rollback is
safe. This requirement does not introduce a universal database migration or
workflow engine.

** Global non-goals

The following constraints keep Gesso's semantic and proof boundary deliberately
smaller than the set of all possible distributed-system machinery.

Applications may opt into stronger mechanisms when their domain benefits.

Gesso does not require those mechanisms universally.

*** No universal persisted fact DAG

Gesso does not require a second persisted fact graph containing every:

- authoritative fact;
- provisional fact;
- supersession relation;
- command;
- resolution;
- dependency edge.

Fact-graph concepts are useful for reasoning.

Application models and their database remain the persistence authority for domain
facts and any domain history they choose to retain.

*** No universal Git-like history

Gesso does not require every model to persist:

- branches;
- merge commits;
- common ancestors;
- three-way history;
- universal conflict records.

The Git analogy is useful for reasoning about:

#+begin_example
authoritative basis
provisional local state
semantic commands
re-evaluation
reconciliation
#+end_example

Gesso may provide reusable reconciliation machinery.

The model provides the meaning of reconciliation.

Applications remain free to adopt stronger domain histories such as:

- event sourcing;
- CRDTs;
- ancestry;
- manual reconciliation workflows;
- base/ours/theirs resolution.

*** No universal global integer revision

Gesso does not invent one global monotonically increasing integer that stands
for all authoritative application state.

That would incorrectly collapse:

- XTDB progression;
- model/entity revision;
- valid/effective time;
- command causality;
- actor knowledge frontier.

Models may define their own integer revisions when domain semantics benefit.

*** No universal cross-node participant-message fabric

Durable cross-user facts normally rendezvous through authoritative model state.

A global runtime directory mapping every actor to a current Aleph node is not a
baseline requirement.

Direct cross-node participant routing is added only for protocols whose message
receipt is itself semantically required.

*** No universal durable suspended workflow engine

Ordinary Choreo does not persist every suspended server-side execution for hours
or days.

Human-scale waiting normally lives in authoritative model state and later short
executions.

A durable workflow engine would be an explicit separate capability.

*** No transparent arbitrary stale-plan migration

The framework does not promise to translate arbitrary suspended browser machine
state from one incompatible deployment into another.

The safe default is:

#+begin_example
detect incompatibility
    |
semantic retirement
    |
best-effort cleanup
    |
permitted authoritative reacquisition / explicit recovery action
    |
current-plan restart
#+end_example

Specific compatibility/migration can be added where explicitly justified.
Retirement cannot be used to silently bypass HumanHelp's acquisition/adoption
policy. Recovery identifies which local choices and adopted membership survive
and what requires a new authorized acquisition.

*** No proof of the entire browser/OS/hardware stack

Gesso does not claim to formally verify:

- Chromium/Firefox;
- JavaScript engine;
- JVM;
- OS;
- CPU;
- network hardware;
- HTMX internals;
- every third-party dependency.

Formal browser-adapter claims are relative to explicit primitive/FFI contracts.

Real integration/fault testing remains mandatory evidence for those boundaries.

*** No inference of correct product policy from syntax

Gesso may establish that a business-rule expression is:

- well typed;
- compositionally compatible;
- used only where the actor has justified knowledge/authority;
- faithfully preserved through projection/execution.

It cannot infer that the product organization chose the desired policy.

A coherent but wrong business rule remains possible.

*** No arbitrary Clojure theorem proving

The formal subset is deliberately closed and smaller than Clojure.

The framework does not initially attempt to prove arbitrary host-language
functions.

Unsupported constructs fail explicitly or remain outside the stronger proof
boundary under a declared weaker scope. A required stronger judgment cannot be
satisfied by silently treating unsupported behavior as an assertion.

*** No elimination of runtime validation

Static/certificate proof does not remove runtime validation for:

- untrusted inputs;
- schema boundaries;
- authenticated principal binding;
- current authorization;
- plan/runtime compatibility;
- concrete progression values;
- external system failure.

Those checks are premises of the complete correctness story.

*** No elimination of adversarial/integration testing

Formal proof does not replace:

- real Chromium;
- HTMX/SSE integration;
- XTDB integration;
- fault injection;
- load/resource testing;
- multi-node testing.

Those test the trusted implementation shell and external assumptions.

*** No requirement for Typed Clojure or unrestricted dependent types

The typed semantic rule/choreography languages are purpose-built closed
languages.

The architecture does not depend on adopting a universal host-language type
system or unrestricted dependent types.

*** No requirement that application developers write proof-assistant code

Application authors declare domain semantics, rules, operations, choreography,
and explicit assumptions.

They do not manually construct Lean proof scripts or internal kernel proof terms
for ordinary development.

*** No conflation of semantic identity with source identity

File path, namespace name, Git commit, line number, and artifact timestamp are
not semantic identity.

They may be useful provenance/diagnostic data.

They are not substitutes for normalized semantic identity.

*** No proof-sidecar runtime semantics

Proof/diagnostic sidecars remain optional read-only explanation artifacts.

They never participate in production transition decisions.

*** No arbitrary unconditional distributed liveness claim

Liveness/convergence claims name their environment assumptions.

The framework does not prove that an unavailable network, dead client, or failed
external system will eventually recover without such an assumption.

*** No requirement that every actor know every fact

Knowledge and agreement remain scoped by:

- relevance;
- role;
- authorization;
- privacy;
- actual behavioral need.

A correct system may intentionally expose different authoritative projections to
different actors.

*** No automatic conflict policy

Gesso may provide typed reconciliation protocol structure.

The model owns domain-specific conflict meaning and resolution.

*** No speculative database portability

Gesso may use XTDB-specific mechanisms where doing so materially strengthens
correctness or simplicity.

Semantic concepts remain separate from accidental XTDB representation details,
but database neutrality is not purchased at the expense of the current design.

** Progress metrics and completion boundaries
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: req-completion-boundaries
:END:

Project progress should track semantic coverage and rejection quality rather than
raw test count or revision count.

*** Useful progress metrics

Track, where meaningful:

- number of certified semantic artifact classes;
- number of framework properties with explicit proof/check rules;
- number of application assumptions surfaced;
- number of trusted implementation boundaries;
- TCB size;
- proportion of downstream mutation classes rejected before runtime;
- number of semantic dependency mismatches caught at the first possible stage;
- number of production plans traceable to a root assurance DAG;
- number of externally formalized kernel properties;
- number of production checker rules with an explicit formal-correspondence
  story;
- diagnostic completeness for rejected mutants;
- number of exposed application operations with complete whole-application
  closure;
- number of real-browser/multi-client acceptance scenarios exercising actual
  production artifacts.

Raw test count is not a primary progress metric.

Each coverage metric needs a stable denominator: the required capability set,
mutation classes, artifact classes, or theorem scope it measures. Reports retain
missing and blocked items instead of improving percentages by dropping them.
Count required usable operations separately from exposed closed operations.

Assertion counts and TCB size are audit aids, not scalar confidence scores.
One central unproved premise may dominate many derived facts. Report the claims
that depend on it. Rejection quality includes an appropriate first boundary,
repairable diagnostics, and paired valid cases; rejecting every program is not
progress.

*** Operational application completion
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: milestone-operational-application
:END:

The first bounded completion milestone is the usable, structurally closed Gesso
and HumanHelp proving application.

This milestone requires, at minimum:

- the supported final Biff lifecycle substrate;
- authoritative production-model ownership with explicit FX value flow;
- correct XTDB progression/consistency integration;
- executable Choreo, projection, browser, optimism, continuity, and Live
  semantics for the supported operational subset;
- production execution of the same canonical =ExecutablePlan= whose semantics
  are checked and tested;
- mandatory whole-application assembly and rendering checks on the supported
  production path;
- exact current browser-artifact correspondence;
- no silent semantic downgrade;
- required HumanHelp creation, details, response, lifecycle, search, sorting,
  visibility, advisory, explicit Refresh, continuity, and authorization
  behavior;
- real Chromium acceptance using actual controls, routes, generated artifacts,
  and multiple authenticated contexts where the behavior is multi-client;
- representative generic adversarial and controlled-defect tests demonstrating
  that the production path invokes the promised checks.

All required HumanHelp capabilities are interpreted by Part VIII, independently
of the implementation's exposed-action list. Claim, Unclaim, Mark on the way,
Complete, Cancel, and Reassign require eligible positive browser scenarios;
state-dependent unavailability is additional evidence, not a substitute.

The response-flow contract at [[#decision-humanhelp-response-flow]] and remote
lifecycle policy at [[#decision-humanhelp-remote-lifecycle-adoption]] must be
selected and tested before full completion. Refresh action scope, concurrent
advisory handling, and successor-read permission must be explicit under
[[#req-humanhelp-refresh-coverage]]. These decisions belong to HumanHelp; Gesso
does not choose them to make a gate pass.

Evidence separately establishes declared closure, actual production enforcement,
and required user behavior under [[#req-application-assurance-claims]]. A server
starting, no console errors, or HTTP 200 responses establishes none of these
alone. Mutation success requires production-model truth and fresh authoritative
acquisition; a provisional DOM change is insufficient.

This milestone establishes a usable application under its explicitly stated
assurance and environmental assumptions. Until all required gates pass, a partial
checkpoint must name the capabilities or decisions still missing.

It does not claim completion of compositional certification, external formal
verification, or production multi-node realization.

*** Compositional certification completion
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: milestone-compositional-certification
:END:

The compositional-certification milestone requires the intended semantic
interfaces and dependency chain to be operational for the artifact classes in
scope.

It includes:

- certified semantic interfaces for the selected model/schema/domain/rule/
  operation layers;
- a closed typed business-rule subset that rejects malformed expressions while
  permitting coherent but product-wrong policy;
- distributed admissibility checks for knowledge, provenance, authority,
  refinement, and progression;
- certificate composition and stale-dependency rejection;
- a deliberately small trusted checker/kernel boundary where claimed;
- proof-producing elaboration or equivalent checked derivation construction for
  the supported classes;
- systematic downstream mutation sensitivity;
- inspectable proof dependencies and repair-oriented diagnostics;
- at least one substantial HumanHelp operation certified through the semantic
  and whole-application chain;
- complete required-obligation coverage and subject/derivation binding;
- explicit transitive assumption propagation and checked discharge;
- certificate re-admission after cache/serialization boundaries;
- evidence binding semantic claims to the implementation or generation actually
  covered, without conflating their different identities.

The Claim pilot follows [[#req-humanhelp-certification-pilot]] and connects to
the real operational scenario. Positive valid cases accompany the mutation
corpus. An isolated proof fixture, a root graph containing mostly unsupported
claims, or a checker accepting only trivial rules does not satisfy the intended
production operation scope.

The milestone must name exactly which artifact classes and application scopes
are certified.  It must not imply certification of arbitrary Clojure code or
business intent beyond the stated contracts.

*** External formal verification completion
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: milestone-external-formal-verification
:END:

The external-formalization milestone requires an independent machine-checked
account of the stable semantic subset for which Gesso makes formal claims.

It includes:

- the formalized semantic subset;
- exact theorem statements;
- named assumptions and trusted boundaries;
- soundness, refinement, or correspondence results appropriate to each theorem;
- an explicit relationship between formal objects and the production semantic
  objects/checker rules they justify;
- release/correspondence evidence preventing a disconnected formally verified
  model from being mistaken for proof about unrelated production semantics.

The correspondence evidence follows [[#req-formal-production-correspondence]].
Conformance vectors alone do not discharge a required theorem about production
acceptance or execution. If the chosen correspondence mechanism retains trusted
translation/extraction or runtime assumptions, the exact conditional claim and
those premises must be reported. A theorem for a different or outdated subject
cannot satisfy the current release gate.

Exact finite exploration, bounded testing, and unbounded theorem proving remain
distinct evidence classes throughout this milestone. Incomplete exploration or
an unavailable external checker cannot be reported as successful verification.

*** Multi-node production completion
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: milestone-multi-node-production
:END:

Production multi-node completion requires the semantic host/authority model to
be realized over actual multiple application nodes without deriving authority
from host identity.

It includes:

- physical node topology and routing;
- distributed invalidation/interest realization;
- host replacement and process loss;
- duplicate/lost publication behavior;
- reconnect through another node;
- progression-safe authoritative reread;
- deployment/version compatibility;
- multi-node adversarial, load, and fault evidence;
- incorporation of the resulting implementation assumptions into the root
  assurance report;
- the concrete host-loss, publication-loss, duplicate/reordered advisory,
  authorization, and compatibility cases in [[#req-multi-node-realization]];
- preservation of selective adoption and explicit Refresh across reconnect;
- declared command retry/deduplication and retention contracts where claimed;
- stated capacity/resource bounds and load conditions for the production claim.

Tests distinguish an operation's durable outcome from another participant's
knowledge of it. Current-state reads do not automatically resolve command
attribution, and lost publication must exercise the implemented recovery path.
Progress claims include any necessary user action and availability assumptions.
A load-balanced demonstration without this failure evidence is a prototype.

A bounded multi-node prototype may occur earlier when sufficient executable and
authority foundations exist and when it can expose semantic mistakes cheaply.
That exploratory feedback does not constitute this production completion
milestone.

These four milestones are completion scopes, not aliases for stage numbers.
Scheduling may overlap where dependencies permit, while every milestone retains
its own acceptance boundary. Production multi-node completion does not imply
external formal completion, nor does external proof establish deployment
resilience. A compositional pilot can be developed alongside operational work,
but does not authorize calling incomplete HumanHelp capabilities complete.

*** Completion records and continued applicability

A completion record names the milestone, canonical requirement set, supported
scope, candidate generation, evidence references, test environments, assumptions,
open decisions, and exclusions. Every required gate has a result; missing
evidence is not presumed successful. Required unresolved decisions block the
full claim rather than being buried in release notes.

Completion applies to that admitted scope and generation. Later changes trigger
re-evaluation of the affected guarantees and gates through their dependency
relationships. A previous completion record remains historical evidence; it
does not confer permanent correctness on later builds. Unaffected evidence may
be reused where its applicability is established, avoiding unnecessary full
rechecking while preserving the claim.

Documentation changes alone cannot establish implementation completion. This
specification defines the gates; actual implementation evidence is required to
claim that any milestone has been met.

** Working rule for every future semantic change

For every framework or application change that claims participation in the
provable chain, ask:

#+begin_quote
What proposition does this layer establish, what premises does it rely on, what
evidence supports the proposition, and can the next layer mechanically verify
that the premises and guarantees match?
#+end_quote

If the answer is not precise, the component is not yet part of the compositional
proof chain.

The intended end state is not:

#+begin_quote
Everything is formally verified.
#+end_quote

It is:

#+begin_quote
Gesso knows which claims are proved, which are kernel-checked, which are exact-
finite checked, which are assembly-verified, which are runtime-enforced, which
are conditional on liveness assumptions, which are trusted, which are asserted
by the application, and which are supported only by tests.  Downstream programs
are built compositionally from those claims, and construction stops at the first
semantic mismatch instead of allowing incompatible assumptions to drift into an
application that merely appears to work.
#+end_quote

* Part XII — Implementation order and final invariants
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: part-12-implementation-order-and-final-invariants
:END:

This final part preserves the eighteen-stage implementation order while making
its dependencies, milestone relationships, and cross-stage acceptance explicit.
It is an end-state roadmap, not a progress report or evidence that a stage has
already been implemented. The detailed contracts in Parts I–XI govern each gate.

** Unified ordered implementation stages

The canonical implementation order is the following.

The stages are semantic gates, not revision quotas. Their normative details
remain in Parts I–XI; this roadmap summarizes dependencies and acceptance rather
than defining weaker alternate contracts. A stage report identifies its supported
scope, actual implementation evidence, remaining gaps, and applicable milestone.

Each stage must preserve a runnable validation path for the work it claims to
complete. Intermediate construction may be incomplete, but a passing isolated
fixture must not be reported as a completed application stage. Concrete defects
in an earlier layer are repaired at that layer and downstream evidence is
rechecked where affected.

Work inside one stage may expose a missing prerequisite in an earlier stage.

Low-cost later hardening may be pulled forward when it closes a concrete hole
without obscuring the current acceptance target.

*** Stage 1 -- Final Biff 2 substrate and lifecycle foundation

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Make the underlying Biff execution/lifecycle model match the semantic
assumptions used by Gesso.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- select and record the supported final Biff 2 dependency and use its official
  execution/lifecycle contracts; schematic examples are not API verification;
- preserve explicit XTDB version choice unless separately changed;
- migrate generated/model FX machines to explicit invocation input and previous
  output rather than preview-era merged context;
- audit multistate Graph/FX usage;
- express Gesso lifecycle integration as stable-ID native Biff modules;
- establish a mandatory preflight/admitted-handler lifecycle dependency of
  serving; Stage 6 completes the closure checks carried by that boundary;
- migrate HumanHelp config/XTDB/Gesso Live/Aleph startup to native lifecycle
  modules;
- remove compatibility behavior that exists solely to preserve accidental
  preview semantics.

Gate:

- Gesso and HumanHelp compile/run on official Biff 2;
- no production machine relies on merged prior output in =ctx=;
- lifecycle IDs are explicit and validated;
- Aleph cannot serve before preflight;
- core JVM/Node/Chromium and consistency suites for the declared substrate scope
  are green, with existing unrelated failures reported separately;
- failed startup cleans up acquired resources without leaving an unchecked
  application listener serving.

Stage 1 establishes the integration boundary, not a claim that Stage 6's full
checker already exists. A placeholder success report cannot satisfy mandatory
whole-application preflight or the operational completion milestone.

*** Stage 2 -- Authoritative model and consistency foundation

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Make authoritative model transition semantics, XTDB progression, and trusted
execution capabilities explicit and stable.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- preserve model ownership of business truth;
- enforce preparation / atomic commit / after-commit distinction;
- carry authoritative progression through mutation, publication, and reread;
- distinguish command ID from execution ID;
- preserve trusted authenticated-principal binding;
- expose required/supplied server execution capabilities;
- reject known missing capabilities before first invocation;
- keep stale basis as model context rather than generic conflict.

Gate:

- atomic transitions cannot expose half-state;
- known post-commit publication failure is not mutation failure;
- outcome loss after submission is represented as uncertainty when appropriate;
- crash between commit and publication has the declared recovery path and
  explicit availability/user-action premises;
- XTDB snapshot/progression tests remain green;
- production model operations run through explicit Biff value flow;
- missing =clock=/seed/transaction/principal/basis requirements are structurally
  visible and resolve to compatible provider contracts, not merely matching names.

*** Stage 3 -- Portable Choreo semantics, verifier, and projection

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Stabilize the smallest useful global/local semantic core from which browser and
server plans are derived.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- explicit global and role-local transition semantics;
- typed roles/principals/actors/hosts/authorities;
- knowledge/provenance algebra;
- behavior-sensitive branch checking;
- closed messages;
- explicit operation contracts;
- explicit FX requires/outputs;
- resource and terminal semantics;
- authoritative observation as a knowledge path;
- deterministic derivation;
- trusted assertions remaining visible;
- projection preservation/refinement checks for supported subset;
- portable machine corpus across JVM, Node, Chromium.

Gate:

- supported choreography cannot consume facts the actor cannot justify;
- projection does not invent knowledge;
- all supported message/operation/state boundaries retain runtime schemas;
- production runtime executes generated projection rather than a handwritten
  parallel semantics.

*** Stage 4 -- Browser semantic adapter, optimism, continuity, stale recovery

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Make the browser runtime one explicit transition system whose race safety can be
mechanically checked.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- pure =AdapterState x NormalizedEvent -> State x AbstractEffects= core;
- documented HTMX integration boundary;
- narrow browser FFI;
- execution/generation isolation;
- semantic resource ownership;
- fragment single-flight/queued-refresh invariant, with logical slot bounds
  distinguished from metadata and retired physical-request bounds;
- owner-relative admission for shared fragment/subscription events;
- effect permission revalidation at deferred physical mutation boundaries;
- authoritative-install monotonicity;
- typed provisional state;
- settlement/timeout/supersession semantics, keeping current-state supersession
  distinct from command-specific outcome knowledge;
- authoritative presence/absence;
- continuity separated from domain semantics;
- stale projected-plan compatibility and authoritative reconstruction;
- adversarial event-order/property tests.

Gate:

- stale callbacks cannot mutate newer execution state;
- retired generations are powerless despite physical cleanup debt;
- optimistic state cannot silently become authoritative;
- canonical install cannot be rolled back by stale provisional work;
- same =ExecutablePlan= drives production and proof-enabled tests;
- partial installation or continuity failure cannot invent successful adoption,
  erase unresolved obligations, or expose incoherent enabled controls.

*** Stage 5 -- Gesso Live publication, acquisition, and convergence

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Close the path from authoritative commit to advisory invalidation to authorized
authoritative reacquisition.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- explicit publication after commit;
- progression carried through invalidation;
- managed fragment identity;
- initial authoritative knowledge or explicit acquisition;
- progression-safe refresh;
- authoritative absence;
- advisory versus adopted frontier distinction where applications need it;
- reconnect semantics;
- convergence without replaying every advisory invalidation.

Gate:

- no managed fragment is structurally blank with no acquisition path;
- invalidation never masquerades as authoritative content;
- admitted refresh results satisfy their scoped progression requirements;
- insufficient or incomparable observations preserve unsatisfied obligations
  rather than pretending a successful HTTP response establishes coverage;
- advisory debt does not itself authorize acquisition or adoption;
- successor acquisitions stay within the declared user-action/policy permission;
- reconnect does not grant stale callbacks authority.

*** Stage 6 -- Whole-application assembly and mandatory preflight

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Reject applications whose individually valid pieces do not assemble.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- canonical source-owned =ApplicationAssembly=;
- operation/view/plan/artifact/transport/route/server/model/settlement/acquisition
  closure graph;
- exact =BrowserAssemblyManifest= / generated artifact correspondence;
- required/supplied trusted server capabilities;
- semantic-route collision detection;
- no silent realization fallback;
- static build/start preflight;
- dynamic pre-serialization rendered-surface preflight;
- structured error kinds;
- operation/application explanation;
- startup lifecycle integration.

Gate:

- a statically knowable missing required closure edge rejects construction;
- a contradiction first knowable in a concrete render rejects before delivery
  through the actual production render boundary;
- invalid invocation is rejected before protected mutation; a later render
  failure does not undo an earlier valid commit;
- stale browser artifact prevents serving;
- anonymous HTMX cannot occupy a semantic route;
- ordinary genuinely non-Choreo HTMX remains legal;
- startup cannot omit preflight accidentally;
- routes, providers, prepared server realizations, rendering, and served artifact
  stay bound to one admitted generation or a checked compatible transition;
- paired valid fixtures remain usable while controlled defects fail at the
  intended production boundary.

Release evidence, actual artifact binding, and operational test gates from
Part XI apply here. They are not deferred until the later formal-release stage.

*** Stage 7 -- HumanHelp production integration and rich-board proving surface

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Demonstrate the architecture against production HumanHelp model semantics in a
real browser.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- example app consumes =net.humanhelp.site.model.*=;
- canonical HumanHelp application assembly;
- all six required Request lifecycle operations use the generic bound-operation
  path and have eligible positive browser scenarios, including legitimate
  target-helper selection for Reassign;
- Create Request works through production model;
- creator sees own authoritative creation immediately;
- other clients receive advisory without automatic adoption;
- Refresh adopts only justified membership/observations, discharges only covered
  advisory obligations, and preserves newer compatible local installation;
- live search;
- sort and visibility controls;
- Request details/continuity;
- a selected, independently testable response-flow contract;
- terminal rendering;
- login/principal separation;
- multiple browser contexts;
- real Playwright/Chromium acceptance.

Gate:

- actual Claim control emits real protocol command without test injection;
- persisted state survives reload;
- no missing optimistic contract silently downgrades;
- remote browser does not show new Request until explicit Refresh under intended
  product semantics;
- malformed application fixtures fail at the first applicable construction,
  rendering, or trusted-invocation boundary;
- all required lifecycle and rich-board capabilities pass independently of which
  actions the current implementation happens to expose;
- local creation while unrelated remote work is pending preserves selective
  adoption and advisory debt;
- local hiding is not misclassified as authoritative absence;
- response-flow and remote-lifecycle policy decisions from Part VIII are resolved
  and tested, including concurrent Refresh and successor acquisition permission.

Claim alone is the first vertical slice, not this stage's full completion gate.
A permanently unavailable required operation or an unresolved response flow
remains an explicit blocker even when the surviving assembly is closed.

*** Stage 8 -- Generic adversarial closure and additional reference applications

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Demonstrate that the semantic/application design is Gesso-generic rather than
accidentally HumanHelp-specific.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- small reference choreographies such as checkout, approval, and an ephemeral
  message/presence case;
- forged browser inputs;
- unauthorized reads/subscriptions;
- unrelated versus relevant authoritative advancement;
- duplicate/lost settlement;
- dropped invalidation;
- already-incorporated command;
- authoritative disappearance;
- browser suspension;
- stale plan;
- continuity failure;
- load/fault testing;
- HTMX/SSE lifecycle conformance.

Gate:

- reference applications need no HumanHelp-specific framework hooks;
- adversarial browser cannot gain authority;
- trusted external shell assumptions are exercised in real Chromium;
- unsupported semantic constructs fail explicitly rather than degrading;
- injected failures assert expected diagnostics precisely while unexpected
  console/page/request failures still fail acceptance;
- recovery tests distinguish known rejection, lost command attribution, known
  commit with failed delivery, and current-state supersession.

*** Stage 9 -- Certified semantic interfaces and proof DAG

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Replace proof-by-reputation with compositional checked semantic dependencies.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- inventory current assurance claims;
- canonical proposition/certificate vocabulary;
- raw versus certified artifact boundary;
- semantic IDs for normalized interfaces;
- stale dependency rejection;
- root proof DAG/report;
- explicit certification profiles and artifact-class obligations;
- cache/deserialization re-admission and scoped assumption identity.

Gate:

- same-shaped raw map cannot impersonate certified artifact;
- semantically stale dependency cannot compose;
- at least two independent existing checks can share certificate vocabulary
  without losing their distinctions.

*** Stage 10 -- Typed downstream business-rule language

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Reject malformed downstream business-rule expressions before they enter model or
choreography composition.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- closed rule AST;
- nominal type/field resolution;
- enum/variant typing;
- optional presence refinement;
- relationship predicates;
- deterministic derivations;
- exhaustive closed match/case where required;
- source-aware diagnostics;
- explicit evaluation, absence/nil, ordering, and primitive failure semantics;
- observation- and subject-bound path refinements;
- declared totality or typed partiality for each admitted primitive.

Gate:

- malformed rule cannot become certified;
- wrong-but-well-formed policy remains representable;
- rule use at a distributed point still requires knowledge/provenance/authority.

*** Stage 11 -- Certificate hierarchy across namespaces

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Make schema/domain/relationship/rule/operation/Choreo/projection/plan/application
guarantees compose transitively.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- schema certificates;
- domain certificates;
- relationship/graph certificates;
- rule-set certificates;
- operation/FX contracts;
- core/public API certificates;
- Choreo certificates;
- projection certificates;
- executable-plan certificates;
- application-assembly/report integration.

Gate:

- changing relied-upon upstream semantics invalidates downstream construction at
  the earliest dependent stage;
- closed registries cannot gain undeclared members;
- unsupported dependency cycles fail;
- assumptions propagate per guarantee and can be discharged only through accepted
  evidence with compatible scope;
- semantic contract reuse does not preserve stale implementation evidence.

*** Stage 12 -- Minimal proof kernel and proof-producing elaboration

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Shrink the logical TCB so a small checker, not the whole compiler, decides
whether derivations are valid.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- small proposition syntax;
- first inference rules;
- derivation checking;
- richer elaborator/compiler producing proof terms;
- malformed-derivation mutation tests;
- explicit assertions as leaves rather than fake proofs;
- complete required-obligation coverage tied to the normalized subject;
- explicit normalization/projection/lowering correspondence or trusted premises;
- rejection of omitted obligations, wrong-subject proofs, and false assumption
  discharge, not merely malformed submitted derivations.

Gate:

- deliberately buggy elaborator output cannot convince kernel of invalid
  conclusion;
- kernel responsibilities remain small and inspectable;
- diagnostic/proof sidecars remain runtime-nonsemantic;
- incomplete checking, timeout, and unsupported features cannot yield successful
  certification for a profile requiring that judgment.

*** Stage 13 -- HumanHelp compositional proof pilot

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Carry one real production HumanHelp operation through the semantic proof DAG and
application realization report.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- Request/Membership/Organization semantic dependencies where actually needed;
- Claim rule/operation contract;
- Choreo certificate;
- projection;
- =ExecutablePlan=;
- application assembly/artifact realization;
- explicit application assumptions;
- adversarial upstream/downstream mutants.

Gate:

- upstream semantic change prevents stale Claim plan construction;
- root report distinguishes proved, assembly-verified, runtime, asserted, and
  trusted claims;
- real runtime still uses the same executable artifact;
- the certificate chain connects to the actual Claim control, trusted model
  transition, and authoritative reacquisition exercised in Part VIII;
- omission of an obligation or substitution of an implementation/artifact cannot
  preserve an inapplicable success report.

*** Stage 14 -- Systematic downstream mutation campaign

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Demonstrate rejection quality across every supported proof/composition rule.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- hand-authored diagnostic mutants;
- generated one-step mutants;
- schema/domain mutations;
- rule/type mutations;
- knowledge/provenance mutations;
- authority mutations;
- choreography/projection mutations;
- certificate/staleness/forgery mutations;
- application-closure mutations.

Gate:

- every supported rule's applicable rejection conditions have negative cases
  with expected outcomes fixed before execution, alongside valid controls;
- intrinsically valid changes that break an old dependency reject at composition,
  while accepted-by-design policy variations retain their explicit classification;
- incomplete test execution is not counted as either a successful rejection or
  an intentional acceptance;
- root failure is not obscured by later secondary "missing plan" damage;
- structured diagnostics identify expected versus actual evidence.

*** Stage 15 -- Explainability and repair surface

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Make the proof/application state inspectable enough that humans and LLMs can
repair local failures without reverse-engineering the entire runtime.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- explain operation;
- explain knowledge/provenance derivation;
- explain application closure path;
- explain stale dependency;
- open-obligation report;
- stable structured diagnostics;
- source ownership;
- valid candidate alternatives for closed registries.

Gate:

- malformed fixtures expose the failed obligation, actual/required evidence,
  responsible source, and appropriate repair boundary;
- suggested alternatives preserve declared semantics; diagnostics do not claim
  a uniquely correct business-policy repair where the product must decide;
- explanation is read-only and cannot affect semantic execution.

*** Stage 16 -- Independent formalization of the stable small kernel

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Machine-check soundness of production-used logical rules outside the Clojure
implementation that proposes them.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- formal proposition/type semantics;
- knowledge/provenance algebra;
- capability/authority entailment;
- progression algebra;
- certificate composition;
- selected transition safety;
- soundness theorem for the selected production-used rule class;
- explicit assumptions, admitted axioms, supported subset, and formal-tool version;
- stated proof scope, with finite exploration and unbounded claims distinguished.

Gate:

- at least one production-used certificate class relies on inference rules with
  externally machine-checked soundness;
- the report distinguishes soundness of those formal rules from correspondence
  of the production checker, which Stage 17 must establish at its claimed level.

*** Stage 17 -- Production/formal correspondence and release integration

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Connect production certificate acceptance to the externally formalized rules and
make stale proof/application artifacts impossible to ship accidentally.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- choose an explicit correspondence method and record its actual evidence class;
- do not treat generated conformance vectors as a universal correspondence proof
  without a sound exhaustive-coverage argument;
- bind formal subjects, complete obligations, dependencies, translations, and
  actual admitted production artifacts;
- deterministic certificate regeneration;
- dependency-aware incremental rebuild;
- build gates for unresolved obligations/stale semantic IDs;
- deterministic executable plan generation;
- exact browser artifact closure;
- root release report;
- ordinary JVM/Node/Chromium/adversarial tests retained.

Gate:

- release cannot ship a stale certified plan after semantic dependency change;
- formal checker story applies to actual production acceptance rather than a
  disconnected model;
- proof sidecars remain excluded from production semantics;
- required unavailable or incomplete checking blocks the requested admission
  without silently weakening its assurance profile.

This stage strengthens the operational release discipline already required by
Stages 1–8. It does not introduce artifact freshness, real-browser gates, or
production-path enforcement for the first time.

*** Stage 18 -- Multi-node realization

Goal:

#+begin_quote
Realize the already-defined host/authority/invalidation semantics across several
actual application nodes.
#+end_quote

Required work:

- multiple Aleph nodes;
- concrete routing/load balancing;
- distributed Live invalidation/interest implementation;
- host replacement;
- duplicate/lost publication behavior;
- reconnect through another node;
- process loss;
- progression-safe authoritative reread;
- multi-node adversarial/load/fault tests;
- update root assumptions/contracts from actual implementation evidence.

Gate:

- physical node replacement never changes semantic authority or command identity;
- node-local state may disappear without losing authoritative model truth;
- users on different nodes converge through authority under the declared
  acquisition/adoption policy, recovery actions, and environmental premises;
- retry/duplicate handling follows the model's declared durable identity and
  retention contract where claimed;
- crash after commit before publication exercises the actual recovery path;
- another-node current-state reads do not invent command attribution;
- reconnect preserves authenticated principal binding and selective adoption;
- direct participant messaging, if added, remains semantically distinct from
  advisory invalidation;
- multi-node assumptions are incorporated into the root assurance report.

** Stage dependency summary

The numbered stages express a default delivery order and a useful accumulation
of evidence.  They are not a universal strict dependency chain.

For roadmap reasoning the design distinguishes:

- *semantic prerequisite* -- a later claim genuinely depends on an earlier
  semantic contract;
- *implementation priority* -- work should normally be delivered earlier even
  when another activity could technically begin;
- *exploratory feedback* -- bounded work may be done early to expose missing
  semantics or assumptions;
- *optional early hardening* -- low-cost later mechanisms may be pulled forward
  when they close a concrete current hole;
- *completion dependency* -- a named milestone cannot pass until the listed
  acceptance condition is satisfied.

The default project priority remains:

#+begin_example
Stages 1-2
    substrate + authoritative model foundation
        |
Stages 3-5
    Choreo + browser + Live executable semantics
        |
Stages 6-8
    whole-application closure + HumanHelp + generic adversarial proof-by-use
        |
Stages 9-15
    compositional certification + typed downstream language + mutation/explanation
        |
Stages 16-17
    independent formalization + production correspondence/release

After sufficient executable/authority foundations exist:
    \--> bounded multi-node prototype may provide exploratory feedback

Stage 18:
    production multi-node realization under its own acceptance requirements
#+end_example

This diagram states delivery priority, not that every theorem in Stages 16-17 is
a semantic prerequisite for learning from a small real multi-node prototype.
Nor does early topology exploration waive any requirement of production
multi-node completion.

Low-cost certificate/identity hardening may be added earlier when it helps close
Stages 1-8.  Full proof-system expansion should not delay making the actual
application structurally closed and usable.

If exploratory multi-node work exposes a missing distinction in identity,
authority, progression, invalidation, or recovery, the semantic design is
corrected at its owning layer and downstream proof assumptions are updated.  The
prototype does not gain authority merely because it exists earlier in the
schedule.

*** Milestone relationship

| Completion scope | Principal stage evidence | Additional requirement |
|------------------+--------------------------+------------------------|
| Operational application | Stages 1–8 | All [[#milestone-operational-application][Part XI operational gates]], including complete required HumanHelp behavior |
| Compositional certification | Stages 9–15 over the executable foundation | [[#milestone-compositional-certification][Named certified scope]], real Claim pilot, full obligations and explicit assumptions |
| External formal verification | Stages 16–17 over the selected stable certification scope | [[#milestone-external-formal-verification][Applicable production correspondence]], not only a theorem about a separate model |
| Production multi-node | Stage 18 over sufficient operational foundations | [[#milestone-multi-node-production][Real topology and fault evidence]]; external formal completion is not a universal prerequisite |

The table is navigational. Part XI owns the completion requirements. A stage's
partial implementation or exploratory prototype is not a substitute for those
gates. Scheduling can overlap while the evidence needed for a claim remains
mandatory.

*** Avoiding apparent dependency cycles

Early stages may establish integration seams whose full checks are implemented
later: the mandatory lifecycle preflight boundary precedes full application
closure, and an initial semantic verifier precedes the smaller proof-producing
kernel. Reports state exactly what exists at each checkpoint. Placeholder checks
cannot be labeled as the final stronger guarantee.

Likewise, explainability, release integrity, and controlled-defect tests begin
when their first relevant production boundary exists. Their later dedicated
stages expand coverage; they do not permit earlier work to ship silently broken
or unreviewable. A new failure class may send work back to its owning semantic
layer without creating a second application-specific workaround.

** Cross-stage acceptance gates

Several gates apply repeatedly across the roadmap.

*** Gate A -- no silent downgrade

If source declares stronger semantics, missing realization data rejects or
renders explicitly unavailable.

It never silently weakens into ordinary transport semantics. Explicit
unavailability can be structurally valid while still failing a milestone that
requires the capability to be usable.

*** Gate B -- same semantic object in proof, test, and production

The object whose properties are reasoned about is the object production executes
or consumes.

No richer test-only semantic machine substitutes for production. Where a formal
object is a different representation, the declared checked correspondence or
explicit trust premise must connect it to the actual production object.

*** Gate C -- source ownership remains single

Semantic facts have one owner.

Other layers derive or validate.

They do not maintain plausibly synchronized copies.

*** Gate D -- security remains trusted-side

No browser-supplied role, principal, certificate, plan, basis, provenance, or
DOM state can increase server authority.

*** Gate E -- exact artifact correspondence

Diagnostic/proof/artifact correspondence is exact where exactness is claimed.

No timestamp/name/best-effort guessing.

*** Gate F -- dynamic facts remain dynamic

Preflight does not pretend to prove current authorization, network availability,
or future environment events.

*** Gate G -- real browser remains a release gate

Formal semantic work does not eliminate real Chromium/HTMX integration testing.

*** Gate H -- malformed downstream programs stop early

Once enough information exists to know a structural contradiction, later
runtime layers are not allowed to "try anyway."

*** Gate I -- representation is not admission

A same-shaped map, nominal-looking value, discriminator key, deserialized record,
or browser-supplied label cannot acquire stronger semantic status merely by
representation.

Where stronger status matters, the supported path must establish it through the
appropriate checker, trusted acquisition, authorization, certificate-validation,
or other declared admission boundary.  Nominal or opaque types may make that
boundary harder to misuse accidentally, but their names are not proofs and they
do not create runtime authority.

*** Gate J -- independent capability completeness

A closed graph cannot redefine the product by omission. Required capabilities
and their positive outcome tests remain independent of the exposed-operation
registry. Unresolved required product choices block the affected full milestone.

*** Gate K -- participant knowledge and authoritative outcome remain distinct

A timeout or retired execution does not prove a submitted command failed.
Current-state acquisition can supersede provisional presentation without proving
which command produced that state. Recovery and diagnostics preserve the
remaining uncertainty.

*** Gate L -- scope and permission survive recovery

Advisory requirements, observations, and adoption are scoped. A newer local
result cannot admit unrelated remote Requests or erase their outstanding debt.
Queued successors, reconnect, and stale-plan recovery require their declared
acquisition/adoption permission.

*** Gate M -- evidence scope survives composition and release

Every claim retains its subject, assumptions, evidence class, and applicable
implementation/generation. Omitted obligations, copied identities, incomplete
checks, or tests on a different artifact cannot establish the requested gate.
Finite models and conformance vectors retain the limits of their actual evidence.

** Final unified design principle

The unified design is governed by the following final principle:

#+begin_quote
A Gesso choreography is a typed description of how roles acting for principals
interact with authoritative and evolving facts.

Projection produces local machines whose behavior is justified by available
knowledge. Knowledge may arise through declared inputs, communication,
authoritative observation, deterministic derivation, or explicit trusted
assertion; communication is one knowledge-producing mechanism rather than the
definition of distributed knowledge.

HumanHelp models own semantic truth and atomic authoritative transitions.

Durable cross-user facts normally rendezvous through model authority. Gesso Live
transports advisory signals that relevant authority changed, and authoritative
rereads advance scoped actor knowledge when acquisition/adoption is permitted,
without requiring every observer to receive a dedicated participant message.
An invalidation does not itself authorize automatic visible adoption.

Concrete Aleph nodes are interchangeable hosts for logical authority
projections. Node-local runtime state may disappear without changing
authoritative model truth. Command retry and publication recovery require their
explicit contracts; physical host interchangeability does not supply exactly-
once effects or knowledge of a lost command outcome.

Optimistic state is typed provisional knowledge derived from a known
authoritative basis and semantic command.

Views attach typed operations to affordances and determine presentation.

The browser runtime realizes Gesso semantics through a small pure transition
core whose safety/refinement properties are machine checked. HTMX remains the
ordinary declarative owner of its request/response/OOB/swap/settle lifecycle and
is gated or observed only through documented lifecycle boundaries where Gesso
semantics require it; a custom HTMX extension is not an architectural
requirement. Gesso-owned timers, provisional DOM work, continuity resources, and
other direct browser effects form a deliberately narrow FFI/effect shell. Both
boundaries are explicit proof assumptions exercised by real Chromium integration
tests.

The browser is adversarial. Client-provided roles, principals, authority labels,
projected states, capabilities, basis claims, scopes, or provenance cannot grant
server authority. Trusted server projections bind authenticated principals,
re-authorize reads and writes, and establish durable application facts through
public model operations, normally realized with XTDB.

Gesso may assume XTDB wherever doing so materially simplifies correctness; it
does not pay speculative complexity for database portability while still
keeping semantic concepts such as authority distinct from accidental XTDB
representation details.

Incompatible stale projections retire and follow declared recovery or permitted
authoritative reconstruction rather than undefined continuation, while continuity
remains a separate local rendering concern. Recovery does not silently waive the
application's selective adoption policy. Semantic retirement/generation invalidation makes stale work
powerless immediately; physical browser cleanup is best effort and is not a
precondition for new logical ownership.

The proved language grows only when a concrete Gesso failure class or required
application behavior justifies the additional proof complexity. Usable HumanHelp
capabilities, production enforcement, and assembly closure remain independent
acceptance claims; a more sophisticated proof system cannot compensate for an
omitted required action or an unchecked production path.

Representations, nominal categories, admitted semantic values, certificates,
and proofs remain distinct.  Gesso may use nominal or opaque values where they
prevent consequential same-shaped substitutions, but ordinary downstream web
code should remain declaration-first and should not manually reproduce internal
proof plumbing.  Stronger semantic status comes from the required construction,
checking, trusted acquisition, or authorization boundary rather than from a map
shape or type name.

The word *proved* is reserved for properties derived by the formal semantics and
its machine-checked proof/refinement machinery under named assumptions. Tests
remain essential for the trusted shell, external libraries, real browsers,
operational scale, and application contracts, but they are not used as a
synonym for proof.
#+end_quote
